In this and in the preceding volume of the Vocabulary of the Kwáfi notice was made of the existence of the great Equatorial Lake, and Snow-capped Mountains, which subsequent discoveries have made familiar. Burton was just starting on his famous expedition to Tanganyika, when the Preface to the Masai Vocabulary was written. It consists of two parts, Masai-English and English-Masai. Other Vocabularies have been contributed, but no further advance has been made. Lepsius in his Review of the Languages of Africa in his Preface to his Nuba Grammar remarks that there are no Nominal Prefixes; that in the Conjugation the Personal Pronouns are placed before the Verb-Root without Prefix: that there is no Alliteration, no Vowel syllable-endings, Tones, or Nasalization: on the other hand, there are Prepositions, the Genitive follows after the governing Noun, and the Verb is placed betwixt the subject and object. Helot Races are found, who live in subjection to the Masai, and in all probability speak their Language as well as their own. Among them is the Ndorobo entered in Chapter XIII. as a Helot Race. Wakefield and Farler have both published accounts of Routes through the Masai Country to the Lake. I took care that Thomson should be supplied with copies of all available books both in Masai and Kwáfi.

#### *VII. BERTA.*

This is a name constantly recurring in the narrative of travellers, and connected with a great many other names of equal uncertainty, Shangalla, Shankali, Dalla, Dizzela, Dar-Mitchegu, Doba; their habitat is in the low land beneath the Western flank of the Abyssinian plateau. The Agau of the Hamitic Group have a legend that they

&lt;!-- Page 197: Book Page 153 --&gt;

dispossessed the Race, whom they called Shangalla, who then inhabited Agaumidr, and drove them down to the valley of the Blue Nile. They are scattered into fragments, and Beke mentions that Dalla is the Language of the Shangalla of the River Takazzé. Beltráme, an Italian Missionary, in 1853 advanced into their Country from Khartúm, and he found that the Shangalla, with whom he came into contact, called themselves Berta, but all the Linguistic Notes which he made were lost, and we know absolutely nothing except that the tribes do exist, and are neither Hamitic nor Negro. With Abyssinians the word Shangalla appears to be synonymous with Negro. Vocabularies exist in the works of different travellers, Salt, Marno, Beke, Halévy. F. Müller has entered it in this Group, but obviously fuller information is much required.

#### *VIII. KAMAMÍL or QUAMAMIL or FAZOGL.*

Beke in his Geographical Distribution of the Languages of Abyssinia places the first of these tribes on the left bank of the Blue Nile, North of the Galla. The Country of Fazogl has been annexed by Egypt. Cailliaud and Ruppell give Vocabularies of the Kamamíl collected during their Travels, as far back as 1826, but in 1844 a lad from the Country was purchased, and sent to Munich, and placed under Tutshek, who had already picked the brains of four slaves, a Galla, a Tumále, a Dinka, and a native of Dar Fúr. It was discovered that the Vocabulary of this lad was identical with the Vocabulary of Cailliaud.

#### *IX. FUNJ. X. TABI. XI. HAMÉJ.*

Three names of very uncertain sound, but with just sufficient evidence to warrant me to admit them provisionally. The Funj were visited in 1871 by Marno, and a Vocabulary is supplied by him, Ruppell and Le Jean.

&lt;!-- Page 198: Book Page 154 --&gt;

They are said to be dwellers of the Northern peninsula of High Senaar. Munzinger mentions them. Krapf in a letter seems to think that the original Language had been trodden out by Arabic. F. Müller places it in this Group. Marno visited the Tabi, and supplies a Vocabulary; but I find no other trace of this Language, but as he states that it is connected with the Funj and Berta, I allow it a place here. Haméj is also mentioned by Marno, and a Vocabulary supplied. Long alludes to them as of Negro origin, though much mixed with the Arab, and their Language much affected by Arabic. F. Müller admits them to this Group.

#### *XII. GOLO. XIII. KREJ. XIV. SEHRE.*

The Golo are attested by higher evidence. Schweinfurth came upon them in his expedition, and gives a considerable Vocabulary, and describes them at some length: it must be noted that nearly all the collected Materials of this traveller perished on the occasion of a fire at his encampment. The Golo are the remnant of a tribe all but destroyed by the Slave Trade: they are found in 8th North Latitude, betwixt the Rivers Kuru and Pongo, near the Bahr al Ghazal, and Bahr al Arab, East of Dar Fertít. They are in what is known as the Seriba Country, the slave-hunting grounds of the Arabs. In their manner and general appearance they are like their neighbours to the East, the Bongo; but the Language of these last, who are pure Negroes, has nothing in common. Ruppell supplied a Vocabulary. The Language is marked by an abundance of sibilants and nasals, and there are other features, which give a distinct individuality to this Language. F. Müller has upon these statements placed them in this Group. The Krej are the inhabitants of Dar Fertít, betwixt the 7th and 8th of North Latitude, the great Reservoir of the Slave Trade. Fertít is the term by which the Arabs and the people of Dar Fúr distinguish

&lt;!-- Page 199: Book Page 155 --&gt;

the Krej: the Baggara Arabs are their nearest neighbours on the North, but on the other sides are tribes only dimly known by name. Schweinfurth can scarcely be said to have visited them, but he supplies a Vocabulary; Ruppell does so also: F. Müller places them in this Group. South of the Krej Country is Dar Fertít, and mixed with the Golo are the Sehre. Schweinfurth describes them in his Narrative: the women wore leaves in lieu of garments, which in the rare cases where it occurs in India is deemed the lowest stage of degradation, but in Africa seems to be the first step in progress upwards, for among many tribes both male and female are totally nude, and it is one of the experiences of a Missionary to have to teach and preach to a concourse of men and women in the simplest form of Nature. These Sehre have a striking resemblance to the Nyam-Nyam, but are not tattooed: they were once slaves, or perhaps Helots under the Nyam-Nyam, but have migrated, keeping their own Language, which in many points resembles the Nyam-Nyam Language, which some of them can speak. Schweinfurth gives no Vocabulary. I cannot hesitate to admit this Language. F. Müller does not notice it. I place it in this Group, because it has affinity with the Nyam-Nyam.

#### *XV. NYAM-NYAM or SANDEH.*

These Cannibals were introduced to the world in the pages of Schweinfurth: they are said to amount to one million, and their Language appears to have several Dialects. They are called Nyam-Nyam by the Negro Dinka, Sandeh, or Zandeh by the Arabs of the Sudán, Manyanya by the Negro Bongo, O'Madyaka by the Negro Dyur, Babúngera by the Monbutto of this Group, and Makkarakka by the Negro Miltu. Schweinfurth was some time in the kingdom of King Munza: he met many of this tribe in his Travels: he gives a Vocabulary and some Sentences, and he appears to have studied the Language

&lt;!-- Page 200: Book Page 156 --&gt;

with a teacher with some success. In external appearance and mode of dressing the hair they resemble the Cannibal Fan, who will be mentioned in the Western Branch of the Bántu family, which affinity opens out many great questions. The word Nyam-Nyam in the Dinka Language means "a great eater," alluding to their Cannibal propensities. Long gives a Vocabulary as Makkarakka: Petherick also gives a Vocabulary. F. Müller places it in this Group. Baker met some of them as ivory porters, and calls them Makkarika. Gordon Pasha made over a young girl of this tribe to the Roman Catholic Priests at El-Obeid: she spoke the Language of her tribe, and she knew another, but did not speak it, called Den-Kaitra, which I have not traced.

#### *XVI. MONBUTTO.*

The habitat of this tribe is in the heart of Africa upon the limits of the Unknown. Schweinfurth penetrated to it, and has been followed by Junker. All Schweinfurth's Grammatical Notes, made on the spot, perished in the conflagration of his camp. The River Welle flows through their Territory on its unknown course Westward: the people are Cannibals, not from lack of food, but from pleasure. The A-Banga are a tribe who occupy a transition position betwixt the Nyam-Nyam and the Monbutto, both in habits and Language. Schweinfurth held intercourse with them, but unfortunately we know nothing beyond the fact of their existence. F. Müller places the Language in this Group. Pott has reviewed the whole of Schweinfurth's Linguistic Notes at great length in the Journal of the German Oriental Society. I can gather nothing of Linguistic interest from Junker's letters, published by Petermann in his Monthly Geographical Magazine, though he introduces quite a congeries of new tribes, crowded together; some are Helot Races, differing from the Nyam-Nyam, to whom they are subject, both in phy-

&lt;!-- Page 201: Book Page 157 --&gt;

sical appearance and Language, but he has given no Vocabularies. Looking Southward we have an unknown Region before us, bounded on the East by the Albert Nyanza, on the South by the Wa-Regga of the Eastern Branch of the Bántu Family, and extending Westward into Regions of which we have no conception. A letter from Schweinfurth at Cairo has given us the latest news of Junker up to this year, and the traveller will soon be on his return. He had reached a great River beyond the River Welle, named the Nepoko, which he identifies with the Arawini confluent of the Kongo mentioned by Stanley. He anticipates the possible arrival of Arabs from Nyangwe into these Regions: if this comes to pass, the secret of this *terra incognita* will be soon revealed, but it is a far cry from the River Welle to Nyangwe on the River Kongo, and we have a long time to wait still.

### *B. FULAH SUB-GROUP.*

It consists of one single Language with several Dialects, and so important is this Language, and so widely spoken, that it throws into the shade the faintly-sketched figures of all but the first of the Nuba Sub-Group. It is a Language with a literature. Most fortunately, it has been fully illustrated by Reichardt in the English Language, in a Primer, a Grammar, Texts, Vocabulary Fulah-English and English-Fulah, and Translations; by Faidherbe in a French Grammar and Dictionary; by Barth in a long Grammatical Note; by Macbrair in a Grammar; and by Baikie in a careful Grammatical Note. The Manuscript of Macbrair's Grammar was deposited in the British Museum. In 1854 Norris printed a limited number of copies, with some additions and a slightly-changed arrangement, for the use of the expedition preparing to explore the Niger. It has been superseded by Reichardt's Grammar. Krause in 1882 sent for my inspection a Manuscript Grammatical Note in German, which appears since to have been published

&lt;!-- Page 202: Book Page 158 --&gt;

in Italian in the Esploratore of 1883. Koelle publishes in his Polyglotta Africána four Vocabularies as Dialects of this Language: all these works are at first-hand. Of Vocabularies there are abundance. Norris, Lepsius, and F. Müller have passed the Materials collected under review. More particular information is required to prepare a correct entry in the Language-map, for this Language is in no place the exclusive Vernacular; in some the Language of the conquering and superior class, in others it is a Lingua Franca.

It is known by many names, Fula, Filátah, Filáni, Fuládu, Púlo, Pulah, Fulbe, Fulfulde; its meaning is said to be "light brown," as opposed to that of the neighbouring people, Wolof or "black." It is stated that Fulbe is the plural form of Púlo, that Filáni is the name given by the Hausa and Arabs, and Filátah by the Kanúri; and that the Fulah, as a people, consider themselves to be on a footing with the White men, or Europeans. The Language-Field stretches from the Lower Senegál in the West to Dar Fúr in the East, and from Timbaktu and Hausa-Land in the North to Yariba-Land and Adamáwa on the Binué on the South. The Fulah spread over these Countries as invaders, and are more or less blended with the indigenous population. They are found in greatest numbers in Futa-Toro, Búndu, and Futa-Jalu, in which they dominate as Mahometan foreign Conquerors. They have placed their foot firmly down in the Land of the Wolof, and the people of the Coast have come under their influence as far as the River Nunez. They are numerous and powerful in Mandingo-Land and in the kingdom of Massina, South of Timbaktu. In Hausa-Land the kingdom of Sokoto and Gando is their creation, including the whole of the Hausa Territory. Far to the East we find them in Bornu, Mandára, Logon, Baghirmi, Wadai, and even in Dar Fúr. Their tendency to expand is not on the wane, and they have made a powerful impression on the Negro population: from the union of the two Races a mixed population has sprung up, called Torodo, Jhalonki,

&lt;!-- Page 203: Book Page 159 --&gt;

Toucouleur, and other names. It is unnecessary here to detail their history or speculate on their origin: their movement has been comparatively of a late date, by force of arms, and coupled with the spread of the Mahometan Religion. Faidherbe knew them well as Governor of the French Colony of St. Louis, on the Senegál, and remarks that there are few free from all mixture of Negro-blood, that they are superior to the Negro, with an intelligence and power of comprehension not very inferior to that of the European. Faidherbe had probably never met a Sierra Leóne Negro like the two Crowthers and Johnson, who are quite equal in intellect to Europeans. Faidherbe remarks that no one would dream of making a slave of an Arab, as he would certainly assassinate his master; nor can any adult Fulah be kept as slaves, as they would certainly escape. Even a female Fulah proverbially at St. Louis became the Mistress of the house into which she was introduced. We are informed that the Fulah of Futa-Jalo are not ignorant nor uneducated, as they have schools at Timbu, where elementary instruction is given in the Arabic Character. Again, we read of a Prince of Sokoto writing a Fulah Grammar in the Fulah Language.

There are five Dialects, if not many more:

I. FUTA JALO.
II. FUTA TORO.
III. SOKOTO.
IV. NIGER.
V. BORNU.

Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of Futa Jalo. Faidherbe admits that his Grammar is of the Dialect of the Toucouleur, or Futo Toro, whence they are also called Torodo. It presents several differences from pure Fulah, and still greater divergences from the more or less corrupted Dialect of the great Central Fulah Empire betwixt the Niger and the Lake Tsad: Faidherbe's Sentences are in this Dialect. The Texts given by Reichardt are

&lt;!-- Page 204: Book Page 160 --&gt;

stated to represent the great Central Dialect spoken in Sokoto. Baikie remarks that Macbrair's Grammar applied to the Dialect of the West or Red Fulbe, the same as Faidherbe's. He observes that the Language was spoken in its purest form in Futa Toro and Massina. In Gánar it is mixed with Arabic, in Búndu with Wolof; in Futa Jalo it contains many impure forms borrowed from the Jalun-ka Dialect of the Mande Language. In the Region of the Quorra Branch of the Niger it assumes another character, being mixed with Hausa, and known as the Fulfulde. This is the Dialect of which Barth has left a Grammatical Note, and that must be spoken by the traveller in Hausa-Land. Many words have been borrowed from Hausa, and adapted to the Fulah Grammatical method. Baikie's long residence enabled him to indicate Sub-Dialects of this Protean and sympathetic Language. The Dialect spoken in Bornu is again different.

Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar, 1881, maintains that it is a Negro Language, and that F. Müller had no ground for separating it into a Sub-Group of a separate Group. I accept the latter as my guide. D'Eichtal as far back as 1841 published an important Essay on the origin of the Fulah, with elaborate Linguistic comparisons with Vocabularies of the Indian Archipelago. In 1841 we certainly had not a sufficiency of Materials to warrant any such comparison, and it may be doubted by those who have passed under review the Linguistic Materials both of West Africa and the Indian Archipelago, whether we have them yet. In the great Linguistic Frame-Work of the World, F. Müller places the Nuba-Fulah Group in the same Category with the Dravidian and Kolarian of British India.

Its first Linguistic feature is, that it has no distinction between Masculine and Feminine, but divides its words into two Classes, everything belonging to humanity in the one as distinguished from all other objects animate, or inanimate, in the other. Its Grammatical method is based

&lt;!-- Page 205: Book Page 161 --&gt;

on *Suffixes*. The Article, which is also a Pronoun, plays an important part as a Suffix; the termination of the Adjective is also conditioned by the Article-Pronoun. In the forms of the Verb it is the Article that provides for the distinction of Number: Moods and Tenses are formed by increasing the Stem by affixation of one or more syllables. A meaning more or less modified can be given to a Verb by Infixes betwixt the Stem and the ordinary Suffix: in this way out of the simple form five Conjugations are formed with meanings conditioned by the Infixes. Reichardt describes it as a rich and complicated Language: it is used by a conquering and encroaching people of great numbers over an enormous area, and is one of the most important in Africa. To the Linguist it is of further importance, as Reichardt remarks, that in its early period it must have been in long contact with the Semitic Family to have been so exclusively influenced by the genius of those Languages in the use of incremental letters. In the interval betwixt the writing of the foregoing sentences and the Revision of the whole Manuscript, Reichardt, who has done so much for this Language, has passed away, leaving on his table unfinished Translations, and Texts ready for the Press, which I shall take care to see properly published, as they have been forwarded from Sierra Leóne, where he died, to the Church Missionary Society.

To Vater is due the credit of discovering the vast extent of the Language-Field of the Fulah. Seetzen at Cairo in 1808 had collected a Vocabulary from the mouth of a native of Sokoto, the Fulah Central Kingdom. He sent it to Germany, where it was published by Vater, then engaged in the continuation of Adelung's Mithridates. When Vater examined a Vocabulary of the inhabitants of Guinea supplied by Barbot, he was surprised to find that the Vocabularies were identical, and the unity of this wide-spread Language, not previously suspected, was established. Years afterwards Clapperton in his journey to Sokoto confirmed the truth of Vater's assertion.
&lt;!-- Page 206: Book Page 162 --&gt;

## CHAPTER XI.

*IV. NEGRO GROUP.*

MUNZINGER remarks with justice that he cannot understand the phrase, "a Negro Language," and that, though it may be a convenient, it is not a logical, method to lump together under one name a lot of people of unknown Ethnological and Linguistic types. The logic of facts must triumph. We have to deal with mighty Phenomena, or a multiplicity of Phenomena. No existing name can be produced more suitable, and I protest against the coining at this period of our knowledge of any new name. Nothing was more illogical than the use of the word "Turanian" in Asia a quarter of a Century ago: it has dropped out of use now, or been restricted to a limited significance: but it had its use, until a more perfect knowledge enabled names of new Families, and Groups, to be struck out. So will it be with the Negro Group. Twenty-five years hence it will give way to some more accurate and Scientific Terminology, as the great mass of Languages, which I now pass under Review, become more understood, and scores of Languages now unknown fall into their proper places.

I admit the great difficulty of the position, but I see no other alternative. Among the Languages thrown into this Group, some are claimed for the Hamitic Group like Hausa and Tibbu: some are declared to belong to the Bántu Family on the plea of their use of Prefixes, and Alliterative Concords. Again it is attempted by Lepsius to do

&lt;!-- Page 207: Book Page 163 --&gt;

away with this Group altogether, and divide it between the rival Hamitic and Bántu; but, ere this process be worked out, very fine tests will be required to settle the disposal of each Language, and there will be a residuum after all: it is safer therefore to maintain this Group for the present, containing more than two hundred Languages.

Besides my course is laid down by the principle, upon which I started, viz. to accept the sixfold Classification of F. Müller as a fact, and then work out the interior of each subdivision Geographically. This at least will be intelligible. I have scores of names presented to me by the industrious individuals who cross-questioned slaves as to their place of Birth and Mother-tongue, but I must relentlessly sweep aside and exclude all those which are not supported by a first-hand Vocabulary, and an unquestionable habitat laid down on my Language-map. Oldendorp, Kilham, Clarke, Koelle, and others did great service; but their statements have been repeated over and over again, until a certain idea of vagueness attaches itself to a Negro Language, and no one can say exactly whereabouts they are spoken.

It is as well to define the word "Negro." The Race by no means extends all over Africa: it is quite distinct from the Hamitic Berber and Ethiopic, though many of those Races are black, and from the Bántu, and Nuba-Fulah, though in a general way all are called Negro: the word is not a National appellation at all, but denotes the ideal type constituted by the assemblage of certain physical characteristics, which is exemplified in the Natives of Guinea in West Africa, and in their descendants in America and the West Indies. Where all these characteristics are not found, the African, though black, is not a Negro in the strict sense. The Race occupies a vast Region South of the Tropic of Cancer and North of the Equator, from the River Senegál on the West, Eastward to the Northern bend of the Upper Niger at Timbaktu, thence

&lt;!-- Page 208: Book Page 164 --&gt;

along the North Shores of Lake Tsad, skirting the Great Sahára, to the Basin of the Upper Nile as far as the Somerset Nile. Of its Southern limits in the Interior little is known, but on the West Coast it reaches down to the Kamerún Mountains. Thus the whole of the Basin of the River Niger is included in this Language-Field, and, as far as we know, the whole of the Basin of Lake Tsad. The Field of the Languages of the Nuba-Fulah Group, is often intermixed with that of the Negro, and in many tracts the Fulah and Negro Languages are spoken concurrently by the same population. The difference betwixt the Negro and the Bántu in character, habits, appearance and religious notions, and especially in Language, is very marked, and it would be impossible to treat them together, whatever theories may be formed of their origin.

It would seem as if the Negroes were the aborigines of North Equatorial Africa, just as the Hottentot is of South Africa. The Negro is depicted upon the painted walls of the old Egyptian buildings; he has suffered in purity of Race and in reduction of Territory by the presence of stronger Races. Other circumstances, peculiar to the Negro, have operated to cause disturbances: the Slave Trade both Home and Foreign, Eastward, Westward, and Northward, has for many Centuries moved the population backwards and forwards, deporting annual thousands, destroying many more in the act of capture, and leaving others, specially females, at the mercy of stronger Races. No Race with less powers of vitality and unexhaustive fertility of reproduction could have sustained the prolonged strain on its resources. A new feature of Ethnological and Linguistic confusion has been the return of the released slaves from Captivity to the West Coast. England and America seemed to have done their duty in landing them at particular points, and the slaves as African seemed to be at home anywhere in Africa; but the united effect of the Slave Trade and its sup-

&lt;!-- Page 209: Book Page 165 --&gt;

pression has been to cause a great extinction of National Customs and Languages. In the United States all imported Slaves, by hundreds of thousands, lost their Language absolutely, and the fate of those that were set free at Sierra Leóne, or Liberia, was the same, and English became necessarily the Language of the Negro population, who, when probed by Koelle gave up two hundred forms of more or less forgotten or superseded forms of speech, except in those cases where a number of the same tribe found themselves together, and even then the dawning Culture of the New Negro, as he may be called, is English in English Settlements, and French in French Settlements. Still the Millions, who remained behind in Africa, kept up their various forms of Language and Dialects, and in some of these attempt is being made to introduce Religious and Secular Education.

Many great Races in ancient time have had their day of greatness, exhausted the power that was in them, and have been broken up, trodden down, or utterly effaced by younger and more powerful Races. But this cannot be said of the Negro Race: they are not broken, fewer in number, or poorer in resources: though pressed upon from without, they have proved to be the only Race suitable to the Climate: their soil is wonderfully fertile, their Minerals in abundance, their power of reproduction exceeding calculation. We know now from the instances of men, who have had the advantages of Culture, that they are not deficient in intelligence, probity, and even genius, yet they have absolutely left not a Monument to tell of the material greatness of any particular tribe, or of an ancient Civilization, as in Central America and Asia, not a written or Sculptured Document: they have but a scant store of Proverbs, and Traditions. No Negro Sage or Legislator ever rose up to show the way to better things, and no Chronicler or Bard has recorded the past. So, with the exception of their wonderful Languages, which are in themselves a History, a Chronicle, and an Essay on Logic,

&lt;!-- Page 210: Book Page 166 --&gt;

and, if rightly handled, serve as a touchstone of what they knew, or when and by what channel they came to know any Science, or Art, or Product, or Moral Sentiment, they have nothing to offer to the Inquirer or the Speculator. Chronic Warfare, absence of Public Opinion, absence of Manufactures, and Seaborne Commerce, absence of any Book-Religion, absence of clothes, and the common decent forms of life, presence of Cannibalism, Slave Trade, Human Sacrifices, Cruelties beyond description, Fetichism and Witchcraft, with the wildest and most monstrous consequences, have been from time immemorial to the present hour the characteristics of Negro-Land. If any ray of Light has shone in upon the Dark Country, it has been from the Mahometan Religion; and let those, who speak with disparagement, the outcome of Ignorance and unreflecting prejudice, of the great Ancient Book-Religions of the World, Brahmanism, Buddhism, and the Laws of Moses and Zoroaster, as well as of the great Propagandist Powers of later Periods, Christianity and Mahometanism, consider what the state of the Pagan World is without these life-giving energies.

Our information with regard to the countless number of Negro Languages (for this Region is by far the most populous in Africa) is still far from complete: it is idle to speculate upon it: all that we can do is carefully to set out all the trustworthy information which we do possess, and set to work all possible agencies for extending it. As we proceed onwards, I shall state what is doing, or can be done. Language is of extreme importance in such a Field as this, and is our only guide for tracking the march of populations of which History is silent. The general tendency of the tribes in Central Africa has been to press down to the West Coast, and this tendency has been exaggerated in modern time by the desire of the tribes in the second or third row from the Coast to get to direct dealings with the foreign Merchants. The attempts to classify which have been made, often upon insufficient data, have re-

&lt;!-- Page 211: Book Page 167 --&gt;

sulted in showing that there are a dozen or more Languages, some of them fully illustrated by Grammars, which are Isolated, and stand as the sole representative of their Vocabulary and Method. F. Müller states, that there is sufficient evidence to prove beyond doubt that the Languages recorded in this Field cannot be derived from a common Mother-Speech, but must have distinct seed-plots. Not only are there such great differences in Structure, as forbid any such hypothesis being started, but, after making due allowance for the influence of Culture transmitted from one tribe to another, there is no such uniformity in Vocabulary as would allow such a hypothesis to be maintained. It is admitted that the great majority of these Languages, if not all, are Agglutinative.

It is impossible to pass unnoticed the opinion of such Scholars as Lepsius, Bleek, Norris and Logan, that most, if not all, the Negro Languages of West Africa are allied to the great Bántu Family, which will be described in the next Chapter. F. Müller and many other Authorities think differently. This is one of the great questions for the next generation, in the fullness of their knowledge to sit in judgment upon and decide. I can venture on no opinion, but in all humility I suggest the following considerations. No scholars more profound, or gifted with more remarkable Linguistical aptitude, have touched the African subject than Norris, Bleek, and Logan; but they passed away many years ago, and none of them had the documents in the form of Grammars and Translations of the Holy Scriptures available to them that are now available. Lepsius' Essay in his Nuba Grammar appeared in 1881, but it seems to date back in its composition to a much earlier period. The question amounts to this, Is there or is there not any proof of affinity in Vocabulary and Structure betwixt any Negro Language of West Africa and the great Bántu Family? and if so, which Language and in what particulars? The great wealth of Grammars and Texts which I propose in this Chapter to pass under Review

&lt;!-- Page 212: Book Page 168 --&gt;

supply ample Materials, and Lepsius himself has codified the twelve salient features of the Bántu Family, to facilitate the intercomparison. Let the Comparative Essay on the Mande Languages by Steinthal, the Grammar of the Wolof by Kobès, of the Vei and Kanúri by Koelle, of the Ashánti by Christaller, of the Ewé by Schlégel, of the Akrá by Zimmermann, of the Temne by Schlenker, of the Mende by Schön, of the Bullom by Nylander, of the Nupé by Crowther, of the Ibo by Schön, the Translation of the New Testament in Yariba by the Lagos Committee, and lastly, the Grammar of the Efík by Goldney, a Language spoken within bow-shot of the Dualla, which is confessedly a Bántu Language, be taken severally, and Lepsius' test applied to them, and then only can a safe opinion be formed.

I have been obliged to devise a scheme for treating this enormous Field in subdivisions, and have finally decided upon the following Sub-Groups and Sections, all strictly Geographical.

It has occupied many weeks to sift through the names contained in the voluminous lists of Koelle, Clarke, Kilham, Norris, Prichard, F. Müller and Stanford, casting to the right and left synonyms, double entries, Dialects, names of towns, and tribes, and, lastly, names which could not produce the necessary conditions for entry, a Vocabulary and a habitat. Latham naïvely remarks that, as a great proportion of the African Vocabularies come to our hands without any notice of the locality which supplied them, it puts the Philologist upon the duty of determining the locality by his own independent knowledge, *or from internal evidence*, and that neither process is without complication. It is time that this method should cease, more especially on the Western Coast. I am more and more convinced, after a long painful study, of the necessity of a Geographical method at this stage of our knowledge. It is worthy of remark that Latham, with all his semblance of sub-classification, at page 168 of his Report to the

&lt;!-- Page 213: Book Page 169 --&gt;

British Association of 1857 states that from Cape Mensurádo in Liberia to the Gabún, he must proceed upon his enumeration exclusively on the Geographical method. It would have been well if he had clung to such facts as no future inquiry could have falsified, and withheld his rash opinion that all the Languages in the enormous stretch from 6° North Latitude to the Equator (including therefore the Yariba and the Dualla) were of one great Class.

No two Authorities agree on the subject of Morphological sub-classification. F. Müller sweeps away Bleek's, as perverse and ungrounded. Lepsius throws aside F. Müller with scarcely a notice. How did Latham act forty years ago? He tells us that he applied the following formula. Is such an African Language more unlike another African Language than any particular Arian Language is more unlike another Arian Language? This rough and ready method was not based upon a careful examination of the Structure, or of the remarkable congenital Phenomena, as disclosed in the long row of Grammars now available to the Comparative Scholar, but upon Vocabularies collected at different times by chance travellers of different European Nations, and conveyed to paper on different methods of Transliteration. Having laid before the Student the Materials for further study, I invite them to go forward, and taking Steinthal's Essay on the Mande Language, and F. Müller's careful Study of Select Languages, as models, commence upon a new and Scientific Classification, which will at least have the merit of being genuine. Christaller and Goldie have already made some advance.

My course in the first two Sub-Groups will be steadily from the River Senegál along the Atlantic as far South as the Kamerún Mountains, and in no case penetrating inwards beyond the Basin of the two Branches of the River Niger, the Quorra and the Binué. Having exhausted the Languages of these two Sub-Groups, I proceed to the

&lt;!-- Page 214: Book Page 170 --&gt;

Central Group, which clusters round Lake Tsad, and thence to the River Nile, where I strike the Boundaries of the Hamitic and Nuba-Fulah Groups. I am quite sensible of the difficulties which surround this mode of treatment, and I have in the progress of this work repeatedly changed the boundaries of each Field and the Nomenclature, but have arrived definitely at what seems to be clear.

| Sub-Group / Section | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| **A. ATLANTIC SUB-GROUP.** | |
| I. Northern Section . | From the River Senegál to Cape Mount. |
| II. Southern Section . | From Cape Mount to River Benin. |
| **B. NIGER SUB-GROUP.** | |
| I. Western Section . . | The Basin of the Niger as far as Nupé on the Quorra Branch and the confines of Adamáwa on the Binué Branch. |
| II. Eastern Section . . | The Region intervening betwixt the Western Section and an imaginary line drawn from a point North of the Kamerún Mountains to separate the Negro Group from the Bántu Family. |
| **C. CENTRAL SUB-GROUP .** | The Regions which lie round Lake Tsad. |
| **D. NILE SUB-GROUP . .** | The upper Basin of the Nile. |

&lt;!-- Page 215: Book Page 171 --&gt;

### *A. ATLANTIC SUB-GROUP.*

#### *I. NORTHERN SECTION.*

The reader will recollect, that the Hamitic Group, represented by the Zénaga Language of the Libyan Sub-Group, touched the Northern bank of the River Senegál, which divides Race, Language and Colour. This is the Northern Boundary of the Atlantic Sub-Group, extending from the Atlantic as far Eastward as the Watershed of the River Senegál and the Quorra Branch of the River Niger, whence a line South-West to Cape Mount will shut in a Cluster of Languages, and a portion of Africa for which there is good hope, radiating from the two English Centres, Sierra Leóne and Bathurst on the River Gambia, and the French Centre, St. Louis on the River Senegál. Included in this Language-Field are considerable *encláves* of Fulah-speaking populations. The Coast-line is well known, and a certain belt of the Interior: to a second belt expeditions have been repeatedly made both by English and French Merchants, travellers, and Government Officials. The Highlands, which contain the sources of the River Senegál, and of the Quorra Branch of the River Niger, in addition to all the minor streams that find their way to the Atlantic, offer great interest. But there is little doubt that within the next quarter of a Century we shall know a great deal more.

Within this Field all the names, with three exceptions, entered in Koelle's Polyglotta Africána can be accepted with safety, and are identified in the Language-map. Many of the names and Languages in this and the following Section are familiar as Household-Words to all who are interested in Christian Missions, African Discovery, or African Commerce. The trouble has been to free them from the alleged Linguistic Classification, which seems to be premature, when it is remembered that it is based

&lt;!-- Page 216: Book Page 172 --&gt;

entirely on Vocabularies collected from the mouths of individuals who had long ceased to use the Language. Thus I have had to gather together Languages of the so-called North-west Atlantic Group, which use Prefixes, and the North-west High Sudán, and pick out the unclassified Languages, blend them with a great many undoubted Languages, no speaker of which happened to be at Sierra Leóne at the time, and redistribute them all in Geographical order within the Field of each Section. To the Christian Missionary Societies, and to the British and Foreign Bible Society, we are indebted for nearly all that we know.

The following are the Languages of this Section:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. WOLOF. | XV. BAGA. |
| II. SERÉR. | XVI. TENE. |
| III. SEREKHÚLE. | XVII. SUSU. |
| IV. BAMBÁRA. | XVIII. BULLOM. |
| V. MANDE. | XIX. TEMNE. |
| VI. FELUP. | XX. LIMBA. |
| VII. BANYUN. | XXI. KONO. |
| VIII. BOLA. | XXII. MENDE. |
| IX. PEPEL. | XXIII. VEI. |
| X. BULANDA. | XXIV. KISSI. |
| XI. BIÁFADE. | XXV. GBANDI. |
| XII. BISSÁGOS. | XXVI. KIRÍM. |
| XIII. NALU. | XXVII. KANGA. |
| XIV. LANDÚMA. | XXVIII. MANGRI. |

&lt;!-- Page 217: Book Page 173 --&gt;

##### *I. WOLOF, alias VOLOF, OULOF.*

Is one of the most important Languages of West Africa. It is spoken from the Atlantic along the South bank of the River Senegál, and Southward along the Sea-board to Cape Verd, in the greater part of Senegambia, including the Kingdoms of Walo, Baol, Kayor, Sin, and Salum. On the North-East it meets the Fulah of the Nuba-Fulah Group, on the North the Zénaga of the Hamitic Group, to the South the Serér of this Sub-Group, but it has an extension, as a Lingua Franca, beyond its proper territorial limits: it is indeed the ruling Language of the French Colony of St. Louis on the Senegál. It has been amply illustrated by French Scholars, and Missionaries. In all the Collections of Vocabularies the Wolof is invariably found, but in 1826 Dard published a Grammar in the French Language, and a Dictionary, Wolof-French and French-Wolof, the previous year. This was followed by an entirely independent Grammar in French by Roger in 1829, and a Vocabulary by the same Author. In 1855 the Roman Catholic Missionaries at Dakar published an enlarged Dictionary of French-Wolof and Wolof-French, preceded by a Grammatical Note. Boilat published another Grammar in 1858. In 1869 Kobès, also a Roman Catholic Missionary, published an entirely new Grammar in great detail, to be followed by a Dictionary, a second Edition, it is presumed, of the one above cited. The peculiar feature of these successive works is, that each Author ignored his predecessors. Dard in 1825 remarks that no previous work of the kind existed, that the Arabic Language and Written Character had penetrated into the Country, as the people were Mahometans, that he was sent out by the French Government to open Schools, and introduce the use of the Roman Character. He gives with his Grammar Proverbs and Sentences. Roger in 1829 alludes

&lt;!-- Page 218: Book Page 174 --&gt;

to Dard's Grammar, but remarks that his is an independent production based upon different principles, and leading him to different conclusions. He gives some Texts carefully translated and analyzed, and his work seems very practical. Two other independent Grammars followed from the pens of Ecclesiastics, but, strange to say, when, in 1869, Kobès published his Grammar forty years after that of Dard and Roger, he takes credit for breaking up virgin soil; he uses expressions only suitable to a pioneer of a Linguistic jungle about Grammar existing in a Savage Language, and brought to light for the first time by himself. A German Grammarian under similar circumstances would have criticized and flayed alive, and utterly pulled down the works of his predecessors, and raised a Scientific building out of the Materials: an ordinary Englishman, less learned, and more practical, would have saved himself the trouble of a fifth Grammar to one Language, while so many Languages had no Grammar at all. But strange to say, the Wesleyan Missionaries on the Gambia River had never heard of any French Grammars on the Senegál, and in 1878 Fieldhouse published in London a small Grammatical Note of this Language, calling it the Jolof, and treating the Field as a new one, though it had been worked by French Scholars for fifty years, and claiming indulgence as if to the clearing of a virgin soil. Still more strange is it that he and his Colleague Dixon had never heard of Lepsius' Standard Alphabet, though the volume was published under the imprimatur of his own Missionary Society: he spent much time and thought upon the Transliteration; Dixon, who translated the Gospel of St. Matthew, was not satisfied with Fieldhouse's Vowel system, and devised one of his own. So the Grammar and Translation have each their own peculiar form of the Roman alphabet under the name of Jolof. It is from the absence of all knowledge on the part of one body of workmen of what the other body is doing, that the progress of Science suffers.

&lt;!-- Page 219: Book Page 175 --&gt;

F. Müller in his Outline of Philology has published a brief but comprehensive Grammatical Note of this Language, summing up in twenty pages its features, and republishing a brief Text from Dard and Boilat. Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar notices briefly, that it is a Suffix-Language, and it may be added that the Noun has neither Gender, Number, nor any kind of Declension. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána classes it as an Isolated Language.

##### *II. SERÉR.*

The tribe which speaks this Language occupies a small *encláve* on the Atlantic Sea-board South of the Wolof, and North of the Mande. Travellers have reported that they are the most ancient people of that part of West Africa, and that their Language-Field has been intruded upon by the Wolof. They are Nomads of simple pastoral habits in the neighbourhood of Cape Verd. In the adjacent Islands of Fadiout and Palmarin the Language is spoken, and the Roman Catholic Missionaries allude to Litanies being sung in that Language within the French Colony. Vocabularies of this Language are found in all collections: Koelle has one of the Sarár, which appears to be a portion of the same tribe settled lower down the Coast. We are indebted to the Roman Catholic Missionary Lamoise for a Grammar, compiled on the spot, and published in 1873 at Dakar in the French Colony of St. Louis. The Language appears to be used concurrently with the Wolof, but the tribe has remained Pagan, in that particular contrasting markedly with their neighbours. Some have been converted to Christianity. Though many of them speak Wolof, yet they have a tender regard for their ancestral Language. The compiler of the Grammar writes with a deep affection for the people, and a long acquaintance of thirty years

&lt;!-- Page 220: Book Page 176 --&gt;

with them and their Language. His Grammar is very complete, and he gives Texts, and Dialogues, and Songs. It appears to have escaped the notice of Lepsius, as the Grammar is not printed for general circulation; but F. Müller writes me word that, in the Outline of Philology, which is in the Press, there will be a Grammatical Note of this Language. When I called on Faidherbe at the Palace of the Legion of Honour at Paris in 1882, he showed me a Grammatical Note by himself, published in the Annuary of Senegál in 1865: he had no spare copy, and I just had time to make a brief Note. Berenger-Feraud in his "Senegambia" mentions the existence of two Dialects; and a Grammatical Note of the Language, preceded by a Notice of the People, by Laprade, 1865, is mentioned in the Chabas Catalogue, but it was so expensive that I did not care to buy it. I am now satisfied that the same work is alluded to; it is called Study on the Language of Kéguem or Kegem. There are two Dialects of the same Language mutually unintelligible. I. Sine, II. None: the latter has three subdivisions, None, Faros, Safi. I was greatly surprised to find that Faidherbe, though he had been Governor of the Colony of St. Louis for ten years, and was himself an Author and a Scholar, had never heard of the existence of Lamoise's Grammar, published as far back as 1873. In fact, the Roman Catholic Missionaries work like moles, and it is only by chance that the books published in their Mission Presses get known in the World. I picked my copy up at the Sale of Chabas' Books. In the list of the Senegambia Vocabularies alluded to in page 27 is one of the Sapi; the Manuscript was lost, but it is described as the Language spoken by the tribes inhabiting the Sea-Coast, and probably is the Serér.

&lt;!-- Page 221: Book Page 177 --&gt;

##### *III. SEREKHÚLE, called also SERAWULI, SONINKE, SERACOLET, AZERIYE, ASWÁNEK, ASSUANEK.*

This tribe occupies with other places a tract called Gadsága on the left bank of the River Senegál, near the town of Bakel, which is independent, though under two Chieftains. On the opposite side of the River Senegál is a tribe speaking the same Language, and of the same Race, called Guidimakha by their kindred South of the River, and Gangára by the Hamitic tribes, to whom they are in subjection. Two other *encláves* of the same race speaking the same Language, called Jafúna and Javara, are found in the neighbouring Provinces of Kaarta and Segu in a state of subjection South of the River Senegál. These Geographical facts have lately been laid down by Faidherbe in the Grammatical Note on the Language called by him by the first and third names on the list contributed in 1881 to the Revue Linguistique of Paris. Beranger-Feraud, in his "Senegambia" describes them under the same names as a peaceful and unwarlike Race, of whom it is hazarded that they are the aborigines of Senegambia: at any rate, they are the most Northerly of the Negro Races. Barth came across them, and calls them Aswánek. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of the Gadsága, admitted by his informant to belong to this tribe: he gives five names of the District where this Language is spoken, three of which correspond to the names mentioned by Faidherbe. The Language is said to be isolated. In this opinion F. Müller agrees, and remarks that the name Serekhúle means *white man*, and that they are not of pure Negro Race, but represent a mixed Race composed of ancient Berber elements, and are the remnant of the old kingdom of Ghanat, which fell under the irruption of the Mande-nga, who held this tribe in subjection, calling them Aswánek, which means "subject," as opposed to Melli, which means "free."

&lt;!-- Page 222: Book Page 178 --&gt;

Vocabularies have been published of this Language, one of considerable extent at St. Louis in 1860 by Faidherbe, and the Grammatical Note by the same Author represents the extent of the existing knowledge of the Language, more interesting than important: it has no Grammatical Gender. The Grammatical Note is accompanied by a Text, carefully translated. It may be hoped that it is the herald of a complete Grammar. Among the unpublished Vocabularies of Barth, Nachtigall sends me word that there is one of the Languages of Azer, spoken in Tivit or Setu, 9–10° West Long., and 18–19° North Lat., which Barth considers to be a Dialect of what he calls Gadsága, and describes as the most widely spread Language of the old Kingdom of Ghanat. Krause wrote to me from Tripoli that he had found an opportunity to study a Language which extended from the River Senegál to the Upper Niger: he had failed to ascertain the name by which the people are called who use it, but the Language was called Ka and Dyenáma or Gyenáma, and that he suspected that it was identical with what Barth calls the Aswánek, and what is entered here as Serekhúle. I have deemed it best to enter three names as Dialects, I. Gadsága, II. Azer, III. Dyenáma.

##### *IV. BAMBÁRA.*

To the East of the Serekhúle, North of the Mande, and reaching to the waters of the Upper Niger, dwell this tribe, of the existence of whose Language we have proof by Vocabularies. It forms one of the four Languages of the Mande Cluster described by Steinthal in his Comparative Essay, and again by F. Müller in his Outline of Philology. Dard in his Dictionary of the Wolof Language, already alluded to, in a separate column adds words in Bambára, a neighbouring, though totally different, Language, and in his Preface Dard alludes to other unpublished

&lt;!-- Page 223: Book Page 179 --&gt;

Vocabularies. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary, and Bowditch, Kilham, Caillie, and Golberry have done the same, and as the three other Languages of the Mande Group have been illustrated by Grammars, and this tract lies on the path of the French from the River Senegál to the Upper Niger, we may expect one from French Scholars.

##### *V. MANDE alias MANDE-NGO, MANDE-NGA.*

It is one of the most important and extensively-used Languages in West Africa. It is the Language of a conquering Race, and has an expansion beyond its natural boundaries, though in its time it has suffered from the encroachment and domination of the Fulah of the Nuba-Fulah Group. The Mande-nga occupy a mountainous Region, but spread far beyond their natural boundaries, conquering and assimilating weaker and inferior Races. Steinthal points out that the final syllable is a Suffix, which conveys the meaning of the people themselves, while their Language should properly be called Mande. Several territorial divisions are indicated, implying in all probability Dialects, or possibly kindred Languages, but it is impossible to be certain. They are Dwama, Sokko, Kabu-nga, Kankán-nka, Torún-nka, and Jálu-nka. In the two latter, the Toro and Jálu of the Fulah can be recognized, which Language is intermixed with, or spoken concurrently with, the Mande over the vast Language-Field assigned to it in the Language-map, which is, however, very imperfectly known. The Mande-nga are a fine and superior Race, and for the most part Mahometan, though some of the subjected tribes are still Pagan. The Mande Language is exhibited to us in Vocabularies by Koelle with four Dialects, I. Kabú-nga, II. Toró-nka, III. Jálu-nka, IV. Kanka-nga, Macbrair, Caillie, Golberry, Oldendorp, Washington, Kilham, and in the Collection of Senegambia Vocabularies: no Dictionary has as

&lt;!-- Page 224: Book Page 180 --&gt;

yet been compiled. Macbrair published a Grammar, composed from personal knowledge as a Missionary, a Translation of the Gospel of St. Matthew, and some Sentences. Upon this has followed Steinthal's Comparative Grammar of the Mande Languages, and F. Müller's Comparative Summary in his Outline of Philology. Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar notices it briefly: it is a Suffix-Language, and without Grammatical Gender. J. L. Wilson has published a comparison of it with the Grebo of the Negro Group and Pongwe of the Bántu Family. Considering the great importance of this Language, it would appear desirable that a new and enlarged Edition of Macbrair's Grammar, which is out of print, should be published, with a Preface defining the Boundaries, pointing out the Dialects and Sister-Languages, other than those mentioned by Steinthal, and the degree of Geographical intermixture with the Fulah; and this can only be done by some competent Scholar in the Field, for the existing Materials have been made as much use of as is possible, and the subject is too large to be taken up by any chance inexperienced Missionary. I had suggested the work to Reichardt, an experienced and able Linguist at Sierra Leóne, but his untimely death destroys all hope of immediate advance.

##### *VI. to XIII.*

A group of eight unimportant Languages can be taken together, being the forms of speech of small tribes, perhaps the remnant of tribes clustering round the Rivers which flow Westward from the Mande-nga Highlands, the Casamanza, the Cacheo, the Geba, and Rio Grande, or occupying the Islands at the mouth of these Rivers. These Languages are Felup, Banyun or Bagnon, Bola, Pepél, Bulanda or Balantes, Biáfade or Biáfares or Jola, Bisságos or Bidshágos, and Nalu. The tendency of tribes in the Interior of Africa is to push, or be pushed, down to the

&lt;!-- Page 225: Book Page 181 --&gt;

Coast, there to undergo a process of weakening and thinning, and to exist, if not absorbed into some powerful tribe from the rear, as a miserable remnant, maintaining, however, an independent Linguistic existence. Prichard gives an account of the degraded state of some of these tribes, and there seems little remaining for the whole eight except to be improved off the face of the earth. Koelle found a representative of each tribe and recorded their Vocabulary, and gives us two Dialects of the Bisságos Islands, the Ankáras and Wún, and it is impossible to deny the existence of the Savages and their peculiar form of speech, as several of them appear in other Collections of Vocabularies, and it is to the existence of such Clusters of obscure names that the vagueness connected with the subject of African Languages is to be attributed. In countries of more advanced civilization such eccentricities of Language are trodden out by great and conquering Vernaculars. We shall find, as we advance, many more such Clusters of names, which swell the lists of the Popular Treatises without adding anything to knowledge. It is unnecessary to add that neither Grammars nor Dictionaries exist, or are likely to exist, or are even desired. They are all Pagan in a low state of Culture. The separate entry by Koelle of Filham represents only a sub-tribe of the Felup, and Padsáde is but a sub-tribe of Biáfade.

##### *XIV. LANDÚMA. XV. BAGA. XVI. TENE.*

Are three small Languages spoken by tribes on the Rio Nunez, of the same type as those detailed in the previous category. Koelle found a representative of each to supply a Vocabulary, and of the two former Corré, a Frenchman at the French station of Boké on the Rio Nunez, has supplied information. They are Pagan, and in a low state of Culture.

&lt;!-- Page 226: Book Page 182 --&gt;

##### *XVII. SUSU.*

Is the Language of the most important tribe on the Rio Pongo: they are Pagan, and in a state of hostility to the Fulah. Christian Missions have long been established among them, which has led to a knowledge of their Language. Koelle gives a Vocabulary of two Dialects, the Solíma and Kisekise, the former spoken in the mountainous Interior, the latter on the Sea-Coast, which is much used in Commerce between the Gambia and Sierra Leóne. It has a close affinity to the Mande. As far back as 1802 Brunton, a Scotch Missionary, after a long residence compiled and published a Grammar anonymously, and composed a certain amount of Religious and Educational literature. It is accompanied by a Vocabulary and Sentences. In the year 1844 I read of a German Missionary Graf, deputed from Sierra Leóne to study the Language as one of great importance, and of a Vocabulary of 1800 words collected, and Translations of the Bible; but I have not come across them, if they got to the Press. At a much later period Duport, a West Indian Missionary, published in London a Grammatical Note. Corré published a Grammatical Note in French in 1877. It appears to be a Prefix-Language without Gender. The New Testament has also been translated. Using Brunton's Grammar and a Translation of seven Chapters of St. Matthew, Steinthal has made his comparative analysis of this Language with the other Mande Languages, followed by F. Müller in his Outline of Philology. The Susu are Pagan, and on the road to Civilization.

##### *XVIII. BULLOM.*

Is the Language of a tribe that is dying out, a few hundred people routed by their more powerful neighbours the Timne. But for the circumstance of the neighbourhood of the English Colony and Free Town Settlement of Sierra

&lt;!-- Page 227: Book Page 183 --&gt;

Leóne, the tribe and the Language would have disappeared. The Dialect of Bullom, however, known as Mampua or Mampa, is spoken in the Island of Seebar or Sherbro, South of the Rock of Sierra Leóne, by a population of thirty thousand subjects to England, while the Language itself is spoken by a tribe dwelling North of Sierra Leóne, though separated from it by the River. As far back as 1814 Nylander of the Church Missionary Society published a Grammar, Texts, and a Vocabulary of the Bullom Language, which is considered sufficient for those who speak the Mampua Dialect also, though other Authorities state that so many foreign words and idioms have been incorporated with each, that few Bullom understand the Mampua Dialect. Schön published a Vocabulary of this Dialect in 1839. Translations of some portions of the Scriptures and some Religious books have been printed by different authors. Nylander translated the Gospel of St. Matthew in 1815. F. Müller considers the Bullom Language to be closely allied to the Temne, and in his Outline of Philology he reviews the Morphology of the former Language, giving prominence to those features which are common to the latter, though, strange to say, they differ in their Numerals. Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar notices the particular features of this Language, which resemble or differ from those of the Bántu Family. F. Müller informs me that he proposes to compile a brief Grammar of the Mampua Dialect.

##### *XIX. TEMNE or TIMMANI.*

Is a Language spoken by a tribe which dwells round the English Colony of Sierra Leóne, a portion of whom, occupying the Quia Territory, are British Subjects. They number 200,000, and are powerful, and are displacing the Bullom tribe. The Language has an expansion beyond the tribe. There appear to be Dialects, the Quiah, spoken in that part of the Temne Country which lies along the

&lt;!-- Page 228: Book Page 184 --&gt;

banks of the River Rokel, the Dialect spoken at Landóro or Lokho, and the Koranko. The people are Pagan: owing to their proximity to the English Colony, the seat of Christian Missions, they are well known. Vocabularies are found in the usual Collections, but the three works of Schlencker, a German Missionary, supersede them. In 1861 he published his Temne Traditions, Fables and Proverbs in English and the original, as taken down by the Author from the lips of the informant in the Lepsius Standard-Alphabet: to this follows specimens of the Author's own Temne Compositions and Translations. Nothing can exceed the value of such Texts and Sentences. The volume is completed by a Temne-English Vocabulary, to enable the reader to test the Translations. The Author translated and printed Genesis, the Psalms, and the New Testament. In 1864 he published a most complete and elaborate Grammar, which he states to be an abridgment only of a larger work by himself still in Manuscript among the Records of the Church Missionary Society. In 1880 he published his English-Temne Dictionary, which is again only an extract of a much more copious work in Manuscript. Translations of several additional Books of the Bible are also in Manuscript among the Stores of the British and Foreign Bible Society. A few days after correcting the last proof of this work he passed away, another of the great Scholars whose loss I have had to deplore since my work commenced. His books are the result of a long and intimate knowledge of the people among whom he lived at Porte Lokko. It is difficult to estimate at their full value such long, and well-directed labours, and to find words suitable to his praise. While his great contemporary and fellow-servant of the Church Missionary Society, Krapf, travelled over different Countries, letting light into many dark places, and revealing many new Languages, and finally, after correcting the closing sheets of his Swahíli Dictionary, fell on his last sleep in 1881, this faithful man clung to the outskirts of

&lt;!-- Page 229: Book Page 185 --&gt;

the Free Town of West Africa, and the single Language of the tribe who were entrusted to him, and made it his own, and it is likely to remain so, for all that is known of it is known through his labours. F. Müller, as mentioned above, bases his Comparative Essays of the Bullom and Temne in his Outline of Philology on the Grammars of Nylander and Schlencker, and Lepsius derives his knowledge from the same source.

##### *XX. LIMBA. XXI. KONO.*

Are the Languages of two small tribes adjacent to Temne, of which Vocabularies are supplied by Koelle: considering how near they lie to Sierra Leóne, there can be little doubt either of the fact of their existence, or the genuineness of the Vocabularies.

##### *XXII. MENDE.*

Immediately South of the Temne dwell the Mende, known as Kossu or Kossa by the Mampua, but the Mende object to the term, as it means "Slave." There has been some difficulty about the Language. Vocabularies are found in the usual Collections, and no one doubted the existence of the Language. A Translation of the four Gospels, the Acts and the Romans, had been made and printed by Schön, and in the List of Books published by the Church Missionary Society appeared a Primer, Grammar and Dictionary, but no copies were forthcoming. F. Müller wrote to me from Vienna, begging for a copy to enable him to find out its position in his Linguistic Groups, but I failed in my endeavours, until Schön informed me that his Grammar and Vocabulary were still in Manuscript on his shelves. I took steps at once to have these important works printed, and they are now published. A few weeks after Schön sent for my inspection a most creditable little Grammar of Mende and Texts published

&lt;!-- Page 230: Book Page 186 --&gt;

in 1874 by the American Mende Mission stationed in Sherbro Island, of which none of us had heard previously. This is another instance how impossible it has been hitherto to find out what has been doing in a neighbouring Mission with no object for concealment. The Mende are Pagans, and turbulent. Care must be taken to distinguish the Mende from the Mande.

##### *XXIII. VEI or VAI.*

Is the name of a tribe round whom a certain amount of interest has been thrown, from the circumstance of their having a separate Written Character, noticed in Chapter VI. They belong to the Mande Cluster, but, like their cognates the Susu, they have left the Highlands and found their way to the Atlantic Coast, South of Sherbro Island and North of Monrovia, the Capital of Liberia. The Language is spoken on the banks of the Gallína River, and at Cape Mount, within the Republic of Liberia. Vocabularies are forthcoming, but all are superseded by the Grammar of Koelle compiled on the spot in 1854. It is complete, and has received the well-deserved praise of German Philologists; it is accompanied by a valuable Preface, a Vei-English Vocabulary, an account of the peculiar Written Character, with Texts and Translations. Steinthal made use of this book with approbation in his Comparative Essay on the Mande Languages, already cited: to him followed F. Müller in his Notice of the same Group in his Outline of Philology, and Lepsius in his Preface to his Nuba Grammar. I may here notice the extreme value of Koelle's three great works: his Grammar of the Kanúri Language, which will pass under Review in the Central Negro Sub-Group, his Grammar of this Language, and his Polyglotta Africána, full of sound and amply-confirmed information by the Geographer and the Linguist. When the necessity of having a General work on the Languages of Africa forced itself on my atten-

&lt;!-- Page 231: Book Page 187 --&gt;

tion, and I looked around for some one able and willing to undertake it, my eye fell upon Koelle, and I urged him to return to his first love; but his thoughts were in a different direction, and he, like me, had changed his Continent, and migrated into Asia. The plan followed by him of obtaining his Materials for the Vei Grammar is worthy of record. He dwelt several months in two portions of the Territory, and took down from the mouths of the Negroes Stories, Songs, and Narratives. These he translated, analyzed, and upon them based his Grammar. Rules have not produced his Texts, but his Texts, caught alive from the lips of unconscious Savages, produced the Rules. They preserve the tradition that they migrated from the Mande Country, and settled upon the Coast, which they conquered: their Territory is exceedingly small. The difference betwixt their Language and the Mande indicates a separation of at least a couple of Centuries. Norris had preceded Koelle in a Grammatical Note of this Language, but without the Materials available to Koelle. The Language is distinguished by an entire absence of Inflection: it is a Suffix-Language. It must be remembered that this Language has a formidable rival in English, which is the Vernacular of the Liberian Republic, and will hardly hold its own in the struggle for life.

##### *XXIV. KISSI. XXV. GBANDI. XXVI. KIRÍM.*

Three small Languages. Of Kissi, or Kisi, we have Vocabularies by Koelle, Kilham, and Clarke; of Gbandi, by Koelle only; of Kirím we have no Vocabulary, and this results from the peculiar method adopted by, or forced upon, Koelle. In his Polyglotta Africána he gives scores of Vocabularies taken from slaves of distant and most uncertain homes. In his Grammar of Vei he alludes to the Kirím tribe actually bordering upon the Vei to the North,

&lt;!-- Page 232: Book Page 188 --&gt;

and speaking an entirely different Language. Further on he records the names of the tribes occupying the tract betwixt the Mande Highlands and the Sea, whose Languages are widely different from the Mande. We can identify all these names but Kirím, and the only supposition is that Kirím is a separate Language, of which he has not supplied a Vocabulary; and yet its existence is too particularly certified to allow of it being excluded from my Schedule.

##### *XXVII. KANGA. XXVIII. MANGRI.*

The position of these two tribes is different. Oldendorp, Bowditch, and Clarke give Vocabularies of the first, with the inexact and yet sufficient indication that the habitat is on the frontier of the Mande-nga and Fulah: it may be the name of a Region in which the speakers of several Languages reside. Clarke connects with Kanga the names of Karu, Barka, Ibribu, said to be near the Temne Country. Mangri is stated by Oldendorp to be South from the River Gambia, on the frontier of the Fulah and Mande-nga. In these cases Vocabularies exist, but no entry can be made on the Language-map from independent Authority. They ought to have been excluded, according to my strict rules: my respect for Oldendorp, Bowditch, and Clarke, all of whom are first-hand informants, has pleaded for their retention, to give them one chance more of being identified. They illustrate the great difficulty of the subject, and the absolute necessity of a Geographical treatment, if any certainty is to be arrived at.

&lt;!-- Page 233: Book Page 189 --&gt;

#### *II. SOUTHERN SECTION.*

In the Northern Section I have followed the Coast as far as Cape Mount, penetrating Eastwards as far as the Mande-nga Highlands. Beyond Cape Mount, at Cape Palmas, the Coast trends to the East, and I follow it as far as the River Benin: beyond which the Niger Sub-Group commences. This Coast is well known as Upper Guinea, under the names of the Grain-Coast, Ivory-Coast, Gold-Coast, and Slave-Coast. I shall have to penetrate beyond the Kong Mountains to the Southern bank of the Quorra Branch of the River Niger, Regions imperfectly known. The first portion of this Field is in the Republic of Liberia; we next come to a few French Stations, the English Settlements of Cape Coast Castle, and the Kingdoms of Ashánti and Dahomé. Beyond is Yariba-Land and the English Colony of Lagos. Through Yariba-Land the Quorra Branch of the Niger has been repeatedly reached, but behind Ashánti-Land no European, and behind Dahomé-Land only one European has penetrated.

Within this Field all the names entered in Koelle's Polyglotta Africána can be accepted, if not with entire confidence, at least with sufficient for their admission to the Schedule of Languages, and place on the Language-map. Many of the names which we shall pass under Review are well known, and the Languages have been illustrated by English, German, American, French, and Negro Scholars. These names have been gathered from the Upper Guinea and North-Eastern High Sudanian Classes of the Polyglotta Africána, the latter of which has been fancifully called by Latham the Kauri Class, with no apparent advantage. The difficulty has been in this Section to get rid of the superfluity of names, and to group the names of Dialects, towns, tribes, and local

&lt;!-- Page 234: Book Page 190 --&gt;

Divisions under one general name. In this Section how little should we have known but for the self-denying and noble labours of a long list of Christian Missionaries?

The following are the Languages:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. DEWOI. | XXI. EWÉ. |
| II. PESSA. | XXII. YARIBA. |
| III. GURA. | XXIII. EBE. |
| IV. BASA. | XXIV. KAMBÁRI. |
| V. KRU. | XXV. BOKO. |
| VI. GREBO. | XXVI. BARBA. |
| VII. BUSI. | XXVII. BORGU. |
| VIII. GBESE. | XXVIII. YOLA. |
| IX. MANO. | XXIX. KASM. |
| X. GIO. | XXX. KOÁMA. |
| XI. GBE. | XXXI. LEGBA. |
| XII. AVEKPOM. | XXXII. KAURI. |
| XIII. ASÍNI. | XXXIII. BAGBALEN. |
| XIV. ASHÁNTI. | XXXIV. KIAMBA. |
| XV. OBUTU. | XXXV. JELÁNA. |
| XVI. GUAN. | XXXVI. GURMA. |
| XVII. KONG. | XXXVII. GARESHA. |
| XVIII. BANDA. | XXXVIII. MOSE. |
| XIX. GYAMAN. | XXXIX. TOMBU. |
| XX. AKRÁ. | |

##### *I. DEWOI.*

This is the original name of the Country about Monrovia, the Capital of Liberia on the River St. Paul. The

&lt;!-- Page 235: Book Page 191 --&gt;

tribe and Language will soon cease to exist, being confined to five villages. The Liberia English-speaking Christian Nationality is too strong for such remnants of old tribes to resist, and they will gradually be absorbed and their Language trodden under. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary, and alludes to it in his Vei Grammar as the relics of a Language.

##### *II. PESSA.*

Is the Language of a small tribe to the rear of Dewoi, of which Vocabularies are given by Clarke and Kilham, and its locality is fixed; but it will soon disappear.

##### *III. GURA or GOLA.*

Is represented by a Vocabulary picked up by Koelle from a lad at Monrovia, who had come there on a visit. It is remarkable as isolated Linguistically from its immediate neighbours. Koelle mentions that the other men from this tract at Sierra Leóne had forgotten their Language, which was spoken, if at all, only a few miles off to the South-East.

##### *IV. BASA.*

Is one of a small Linguistic Cluster called Mena by Bleek and F. Müller. It is spoken by a tribe in Liberia who dwell on the banks of the River Sess, where it enters the Atlantic. Koelle and Kilham give Vocabularies, but Crocker, an American Baptist Missionary, published a Grammatical Note at Edina in Liberia in 1844. The book is very rare. F. Müller mentions that Whitney of New Haven in the United States lent him a copy from the

&lt;!-- Page 236: Book Page 192 --&gt;

Yale College Library. I am indebted to a friend, Means of Boston in the United States, for a Manuscript copy of the whole work made at my request last year. It is much to be regretted that copies of such exceptional works are not freely distributed at the time of their publication. F. Müller, with the aid of the copy lent to him, published a Grammatical Note of the Basa with its Sister-Languages, Kru and Grebo, in the Periodical of the Vienna Academy, and favoured me with a separate copy: so our knowledge of the Language is sufficient.

##### *V. KRU.*

Is the Language of a well-known tribe called generally in accounts of Voyages Kru-men, or Croomen, who are useful as sailors and labourers under Europeans, and who never can be slaves. Sometimes the name is made to include the kindred Basa and Grebo: at present I speak of the Kru or Krao proper, who dwell along the Sea-Coast betwixt the Basa and Grebo. Their Language is spoken in five towns West of Cape Palmas: so much are the words Kru and Grebo intermixed, that F. Müller states that the Hymns published at Basle 1873 as Kru are Grebo. When I was at Madrid in 1880, my friend Garcia Ayuso placed in my hands a small Spanish book which proved to be a Grammatical Note on the Language of the Nano or Cruman by Usera y Alarcon (Madrid, 1845), as a contribution to my African Library. I hardly appreciated the value of his present, until I read the Grammatical Note of F. Müller above alluded to on the Kru and its two Sister-Languages, and found that this Spanish volume was apparently the only Authority, but not the result of local inquiries or knowledge, but worked out in the course of instructing two young Kru boys brought to Spain. Koelle, Clarke, Norris, and Kilham have supplied Vocabularies.

&lt;!-- Page 237: Book Page 193 --&gt;

##### *VI. GREBO or KREBO, GEDEBO.*

On both sides of Cape Palmas, the point at which the direction of the Coast of West Africa trends to the East, resides the third member of the Mena Linguistic Cluster. They are often called the Fish Kru. Vocabularies have been supplied by Koelle and Bowditch. It appears that they have migrated from the Interior and settled along the Coast at a comparatively late period, even after the arrival of the Portuguese, which gives an historical certainty to the Westward movement of the tribes from the Interior. They took to their canoes, and some were capsized and called Worebo: those who escaped, and bounded on the waves, were called Grebo, from the monkey Gri, remarkable for agility. J. L. Wilson, American Missionary, supplies a Grammatical Note from personal local experience, a Dictionary English-Grebo and Grebo-English, and a Comparative Sketch of the Grebo with the Mande, already described, and the Pongwe of the Bántu Family. My friend Whitney of New Haven, United States, was good enough to lend me from the Yale College Library a copy of Payne's Grammatical Note, 1838, and encouraged me to write to Liberia for a copy of a Grammar by the same Author of 1864. Payne also published a Dictionary in 1860, a copy of which was supplied to me by Kohler at Leipzig. Means of Boston, United States, was good enough to make particular inquiry for a copy of Payne's Grammar, and although no copy was forthcoming, he stumbled upon the plates, and had ten copies struck off, with the date of 1883 on the title-page, sending me five copies. I cannot thank him too heartily for such kindness. But before these copies arrived, one of the old Edition was sent to me by Fergusson, the American Bishop, from Cape Palmas in Africa. I thus had the means of distributing copies of this rare book to the Libraries at

&lt;!-- Page 238: Book Page 194 --&gt;

Vienna, Berlin, London, and Paris. These facts give but a faint idea of the many-sided attempts to collect the Linguistic literature of an African Language. F. Müller in the Grammatical Note already quoted passed both Payne's Grammar and Dictionary under Review. Sayce in his paper in the Contemporary Review on the Jelly-Fish-Theory alluded to the fact that in the Grebo Language the personal Pronoun is not expressed, and it is left to gesture to determine in what person the Verb is to be understood. As the Gospels have been translated into this Language, this Grammatical deficiency must have been supplied. How far the Grebo Field extends Westward, beyond Cape Palmas, seems uncertain. A great difference of opinion still exists as to the relations of the last three Languages to each other. Christaller writes to me that Grebo and Basa are both Dialects of Kru. He sent me also copies of Religious Books in the Gedebo or Gdebo, compiled by Auer, who went out to Africa in the Lutheran German Mission to the Ashánti, and migrated to the American Episcopal Mission of Cape Palmas.

##### *VII. BUSI or TOMA. VIII. GBESE. IX. MANO. X. GIO. XI. GBE.*

These appear to be mere Linguistic expressions, but they cannot be omitted: the tribes live in the rear of the three last mentioned, and are represented by Vocabularies in Koelle's Polyglotta Africána, and their habitat being known, they cannot be excluded. Behind them again are the Mande-nga Highlands. Prichard tells us that the Region behind the Coast tribes is occupied by the Quoja, an indefinite name, including probably several tribes. The name is suspicious, and probably a European misapplication of a well-known word in Asia. Oldendorp gives a Vocabulary of Gien, which in description corresponds sufficiently with Gio.

&lt;!-- Page 239: Book Page 195 --&gt;

##### *XII. AVEKVOM or AVIKUM or KWAKWA.*

Betwixt Cape Palmas, unquestionably occupied by the Grebo, and Cape Tres Puntas, which is within the Ashánti Language-Field, is a debateable Region. Prichard, a careful second-hand examiner of early Authorities, says that the principal Nation is the Quaqua, so called from the word used in salutation. Christaller, the greatest modern local Authority of the Gold Coast, tells us in his Grammar of the Ashánti Language 1875, that according to Clarke Avikum is the name of the people at Cape Lahou on the Ivory Coast. Clarke, who is an esteemed personal local Authority, gives specimens of a great many Vocabularies stated to be of Languages spoken by tribes on the Ivory Coast: the Andóne, who live near the River St. Andrews: the Quadya, who live behind: Avikum, Banda, Bukra, Eple at Cape Frisco, Grabwa at Grand Drewin, and Pandan at Cape Lahou: he remarks that at this place the Language changes to the Quaqua, and is different from the Grebo on the one side and the Ashánti on the other. He mentions Wawi behind Kotrahu towards the Mountains, Yukobwe, and Zeribu. J. R. Wilson quotes Malte Brun to show that the Grebo extend to St. Andrews, but does not quote Malte Brun's Authority for his statement: he gives original Vocabularies of the Language spoken at Drewin, St. Andrews, and Dey near Cape Mesurádo.
