So I must leave it, hoping that the future will make the state of affairs clearer. All seem to be Pagan.

##### *XIII. ASÍNI.*

At Cape Tres Puntas commences the Gold Coast, but Christaller tells us that the Ashánti Language-Field stretches Westward as far as the River Asíni on the Ivory Coast. And he tells us in his Grammar, moreover, that in the Asíni or Atakla Territory on the

&lt;!-- Page 240: Book Page 196 --&gt;

Coast and the inland Countries Anka and Afuma on both sides of the River Tando (East of the River Asíni), apparently the Language has affinities to the Ashánti. In the only specimen supplied by an early traveller, Loyer 1701, a few words are *not* Ashánti. In his Dictionary 1881 he adds that he has no reliable information. This same remark applies to the Language spoken at Amanahia or Apollonia, still nearer to Cape Tres Puntas. With regard to Aowin, he quotes the Authority of apparently a native of these parts to the effect that the Language of Aowin is spoken in Asíni, Aowin, Apollonia, Ashánti, and Wasa. I must remind the reader that this is the opinion expressed in 1881 by the best Authority on the Ashánti Language, and we must accept the fact that a Language separate from, though probably with affinities to, Ashánti exists West of Cape Tres Puntas extending into an unknown Interior. Cameron told me that in his late visit to the Gold Mines behind Axim he came upon a Mande-nga population. Bowditch supplies Vocabularies in Aowin, Apollonia or Amanahia, Ahanta and Warsau. Clarke gives a Vocabulary of Ahanta, remarking that the tribe occupied both Apollonia and Elmina on the Gold Coast, and that the Language was nearly the same as the Fanti Dialect of Ashánti.

##### *XIV. ASHÁNTI, known promiscuously as OTSHI, OJI, O-TYI, ODSCHI, ODJII, OCHI, OTSUI, TYI, TSHI, CHWEE, TWI.*

With such a dangerous variety of appellations I can only tender my thanks to Christaller that he has boldly cut the knot, and entitled his works, Dictionary and Grammar of the Ashánti Language. Now, it is a principle accepted in fixing the spelling of names, that the spelling of names which have obtained a notoriety, and been adopted into the English Language, should not be changed, and therefore I write Ashánti. It is quite pos-

&lt;!-- Page 241: Book Page 197 --&gt;

sible and reasonable in Vernacular publications to call it by whatever name the people wish, or to which they are accustomed, just as in India the Hindustáni of English literature is always styled Urdu in native parlance. Now the Language-Field of the Ashánti Language is not conterminous with the political boundaries of the Ashánti Kingdom. The Language extends beyond the Kingdom on the one hand, and other Languages are spoken within the Kingdom. The boundaries of the Language-Field are thus defined: The Rivers Asíni and Tanno on the West, the River Volta on the East, with some extensions beyond, the Atlantic on the South, and the Kong Mountains on the North. The number of names assigned by the older Authorities to this Region, or rather to the Gold Coast and Slave Coast indiscriminately, is very considerable.

Two Scholars, and good Missionaries, who belong to a body of men who neither fear the deadly Climate nor the cruel Ashánti Rule, have enabled me to steer through the maze. Of Vocabularies there are abundance. W. J. Müller, a Danish Chaplain at Fredericksburg, has left us one of the oldest books on African Languages; for his account in German of the Fetu or Afutu tribe among whom he resided, and the Vocabulary which he compiled, is dated Hamburgh 1673, and is valuable as marking the change of the Fánti Dialect, to which it refers, in the course of two Centuries. Protten, a half-blood, published a Grammar in Danish in 1764. Isert in his German Travels gives a Vocabulary dated 1788. Bowditch, Kilham, Norris, J. R. Wilson, Oldendorp, Clarke, Robertson, give Vocabularies under different names, which we are able to trace to this Field. Halleur published an Abecedarium in German. All have been superseded by the Grammars of Riis (1854) and Christaller (1875), accompanied by a Vocabulary in one case, and a Dictionary in the other, Prefaces, Appendices, Texts; in fact, everything but a Language-map. However, Christaller has kindly supplied me with a copy of the Map which has been com-

&lt;!-- Page 242: Book Page 198 --&gt;

piled of his Mission-Field, the renowned Mission of Basle, whose representatives, Ramseyer and Kühne, were not unwilling to suffer imprisonment, and not unready to suffer death, at Kumási in 1869–74. Christaller not only supplies information regarding the Ashánti Language, but the other Languages within the Field, and outside. His Dictionary of 1881 is one of the greatest prizes of African Philology, and may rank with Krapf's Swahíli on the East Coast, and the great representative works of South African Philology.

Before I describe the Dialects, I must explain some of the names which crop up in the first-hand Authorities, and confuse the subject. Fetu of W. J. Müller and Affutu of Bowditch are synonymous with Fánti. Inta and Tafoe are names of neighbouring tribes near Salaga. Amína is used by Protten, Oldendorp, Clarke, but has no distinct meaning, and had better fall into disuse. Kassianthe, Kassente, Tembu, Ijemba, Wáure, Watje or Atje and Ati, occur in the older Vocabularies East of Ashánti, and must be included in this Language. Agua, Egua, Igua, are the native names of Cape Coast, Agouna, of which Vocabularies appear in Clarke, are Fánti sub-tribes. Koromanti, of which a Vocabulary is also given by Clarke, is a town on the Gold Coast within this Field.

Christaller in his Dictionary 1881 gives the following Dialects, as the final result of his experience, differing somewhat from his predecessors, and even from himself in his Grammar 1875.

I. Akán, spoken purest in Akém, and agreeing with that of Ashánti proper; in fact, the Language of the Court and the Government at Kumási.

II. Akwapém appears to have points of contact with all the other Dialects, and is the best suited to become the literary Dialect, being equally intelligible to all the other tribes. As a fact, Riis wrote his Grammar of 1854 avowedly in this Dialect. Barth in his Central African Vocabularies

&lt;!-- Page 243: Book Page 199 --&gt;

remarks upon this, that Riis' Grammar applied to Akropong, the capital of Akwapém, a small District in this large Language-Field; that he laboured in a corner, and had to use this Dialect, admitting however that the Akán, or Ashánti, was the chief Dialect, and the Standard. The Basle Missionaries have translated the Holy Scriptures into this Dialect. Christaller bases his Grammar on this Dialect with reference, however, to the Akán and Fánti Dialects. His Dictionary applies to all the Dialects.

III. Bron (*alias* Burum or Kámăna) is spoken in the Countries North and North-East of Akán, also East of the River Volta. It is inferior to Akán on account of archaisms, and foreign elements: it is spoken concurrently beyond the Language-Field proper by tribes who speak other Languages.

IV. Fánti. Norris, when he edited the Outline Vocabulary in 1841, looked into the subject, and obtained an original Fánti Vocabulary from a Native who happened to be in England, and records his opinion, that there is little more than a difference of pronunciation betwixt Ashánti and Fánti. However, in 1868, Carr and Brown published at Cape Coast Castle a Grammar of Fánti, distinguishing sharply betwixt it and the Ashánti of Riis' Grammar. They were, and had the assistance of, Negroes, and adopted the particular Sub-Dialect of Cape Coast as their Standard of Fánti. Unfortunately they adopted also an entirely different system of Transliteration from that of Lepsius' Standard, which had been adopted by the Basle Missionaries for Ashánti and Akrá, and the Bremen Missionaries for Ewé, all kindred and closely adjacent Languages. Moreover, Parker of the Wesleyan Mission has advanced further in this direction, and setting aside the Translation of the whole Scriptures in the Lepsius' Standard, and Akwapém Dialect, published by the British and Foreign Bible Society, has published a new Translation of the Gospels of Matthew and Mark in the Fánti

&lt;!-- Page 244: Book Page 200 --&gt;

Dialect, and a divergent system of Transliteration. Christaller, as a Scholar, remarks upon this, that the difference of the Transliteration, that is to say, of the mere shell, differs far more than the difference of the Dialect, that is to say, the kernel: that two Dialects can exist side by side without inconvenience, but the consequences of two varying systems of Transliteration are very serious. I am the more surprised at this new departure, as in the Advertisement to the first Edition of the Standard Alphabet of Lepsius, the Wesleyan Missionary Society authoritatively accept it, and under the signature of Beecham, himself an Ashánti Scholar.

As was to be expected, this magnificent Language, thus ably illustrated, has attracted attention in Europe. Pott reviewed Riis' Grammar in the Journal of the German Oriental Society; Steinthal reviewed Christaller's Grammar in the Journal of Philology for 1876. F. Müller has published a Grammatical Note in his Outline of Philology, uniting it with Ewé, Akrá and Yariba, and Lepsius comments upon its relations to other Languages in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar 1880. It belongs to the so-called Volta Linguistic Cluster, which is composed of the Ashánti, the Akrá, the Guan, and the Ewé, which will be severally noticed. The people are Pagan, and it is scarcely necessary to say how degraded and revolting are their customs, civil and religious.

##### *XV. OBUTU. XVI. GUAN.*

Christaller, in his enumeration of other Languages spoken within the Ashánti Language-Field, mentioned these two previously-unknown names. Obutu is found in certain towns on the Coast near Winnebah, and among them is the name of Agouna, of which place Clarke supplies a Vocabulary: whether the form of speech is identical in all these towns, and what relation they bore to the other

&lt;!-- Page 245: Book Page 201 --&gt;

Language, he could not say. In his Dictionary he goes so far as to call it a Dialect of Guan, of which Language we are more particularly informed. Guan is classed as one of the four Languages of the Volta Cluster: it is spoken in the towns of Date, Kubéase, and the five towns of Kyerepong, in Karakye, Trubi, and Salaga, a place of importance near the River Volta. Riis says that it is a distinct Language, and probably that of the fragment of a greater population, overpowered by the Ashánti. Zimmermann confirms this in his Grammar of the Akrá Language, and Christaller, both in Grammar and Dictionary, gives particulars, but no specimens. Of Trubi, Clarke supplies a Vocabulary, and Bowditch supplies the Numerals of Salaga or Nta.

##### *XVII. KONG. XVIII. BANDA. XIX. GYAMAN.*

These are the Languages of tribes who reside North of Ashánti in Regions very little known. Kong is no doubt on the Mountains of that name. Christaller gives its Geographical position in his Ashánti Dictionary; Clarke supplies Vocabularies; Bowditch gives the Numerals. Banda is also called Banna, and is North of Kumási. Christaller in his Grammar and Dictionary of the Ashánti Language testifies to its existence as a separate Language, and to the tribe having been subjugated by Ashánti, and in 1877 to be at war with that power, leagued with Gyaman. Clarke gives a Vocabulary. Gyaman, with its capital Buntúku, is North of Kumási beyond the River Tanno, bordering on Kong. Christaller tells us in his Grammar of the Ashánti, that it is a separate Language, and the Newspapers of August, 1882, report the successful struggle of the tribe for independence. Bowditch supplies a Vocabulary.

&lt;!-- Page 246: Book Page 202 --&gt;

##### *XX. AKRÁ or INKRAN.*

Occupies a very small Language-Field betwixt the Languages of Ashánti and Ewé, to both of which it has close affinities. The same branch of the Basle Mission which occupies Ashánti occupies Akrá also, and Zimmermann, one of their Missionaries, published at Stuttgard in 1858 an excellent Grammar, Texts, and Vocabulary Akrá-English. Several older Vocabularies exist, but are all superseded by this Grammar. A short Grammatical Note in Danish had been published by Protten, a Danish Missionary, in 1764. Rask of Copenhagen compiled a Danish Grammatical Note from information gleaned by him at Copenhagen from a Native of Akrá. Riis in his Grammar of the Ashánti Language 1856 states that the Akrá is allied to the Ashánti in the same degree as the Swedish to the German. Norris, who had studied the Language from Rask's Grammatical Note and the Translations of the Holy Scriptures by Zimmermann and Hanson, a native of Akrá, doubted this affinity. It seemed to him that the Structure differed very considerably. This same Hanson in the Transactions of the Ethnological Society, 1853, published a Grammatical Note based on his personal knowledge as a Native Missionary in his own Country.

I must dispose of a few names. Krobo seems to be the name of a Mountain range occupied by some of the tribes. Tambi is a synonym for Adampi. There are two distinct Dialects, Gá and Adampi. Zimmermann was seven years among the people: he had translated the Scriptures, and his Authority may be accepted. The Roots are monosyllabic, and so few, that Tones have to be had recourse to: where Tones are not used, words are combined to express the meaning. The Language-Field is triangular, having the Atlantic for its base, the River Volta on the East, and the Ashánti Mountains of Akwapém on the North and North-West. The speakers of this Language amount to

&lt;!-- Page 247: Book Page 203 --&gt;

110,000 people only, and the Adampi Dialect has the most. Some small portion of the Akrá-speaking population have migrated beyond the River Volta into the Ewé Language-Field. The Grammar is based on the Gá Dialect, with which the Author is familiar, but in the same volume he gives a Grammatical Note on the Adampi Dialect, which he does not pretend to speak sufficiently to carry on a conversation. The word Adampi means Language of Ada, the chief place, and it is the mother-tongue of the Country. The whole Country has been conquered by the Ashánti Nation, and Gá is somewhat mixed with Ashánti, and yet the Missionaries chose it as the literary Dialect, because they dwelt amidst this portion of the tribe, which had a political and intellectual supremacy over the Adampi and all surrounding tribes. Lepsius' Standard Alphabet is used. Christaller in his Ashánti Grammar gives a similar account of this Language. F. Müller gives a Grammatical Note of this Language in his Outline of Philology, uniting it with the Ewé, Ashánti, and Yariba; and Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar comments briefly on its features.

##### *XXI. EWÉ or EWHÉ or AZIGHE.*

Occupies a vast Language-Field along the Coast East of the River Volta: in a few towns West of the River the Language is also spoken. It was very difficult to get at the facts of this Field from the conflicting accounts, and the great variety of names. I wrote to the Directors of the North German Mission at Bremen, asking as a favour for a copy of their Missionary Schlegel's Key to the Ewé Language in German, and I received a copy by return of post of a most valuable Grammar, Texts, and Vocabularies Ewé-German and German-Ewé, published at Bremen, but dated from Keta on the Slave Coast 1856. The compiler humbly calls his work a Key to open many chambers occupied by the great Ewé people, and he had only been two

&lt;!-- Page 248: Book Page 204 --&gt;

years in the Country. He defines Geographically the Ewé Language-Field, as the West portion of the Slave Coast, bounded by the Atlantic on the South, on the West by the River Volta, and on the East stretching out to those tracts along the West bank of the River Niger which go by the name of Yariba-Land, which is in fact the Eastern half of the Slave Coast. The Northern boundary has not yet been defined. The whole Region is called Eweern or Ewéme, the Home of the Ewé.

There are according to Schlegel five Dialects, whose Fields are of very considerable extent. Many of them are divided politically into small Provinces, which has to a certain extent produced Sub-Dialects, or at least the semblance of them in the lax parlance of casual writers. I. Following the upper course of the River Volta into the Interior, and passing along the boundary of Dahomé, we come to the Mahe, or Makhi Dialect. This is the furthest to the North-East, but its extent into the Interior is unknown to Europeans; as far as we can judge, this is the purest Dialect. II. The second is the Dialect of the Province of Dahomé. III. The third Dialect is spoken in the Weta Province, reaching to the Sea, entered on the Maps as Whidah. This Dialect resembles very much that of Dahomé, and the Province is subject to the King of that Country. Whidah is written Hwidah, Judah, Fidah: the people are called Ffon, Popo or Papaa: the Language is called by one French Missionary, Courdioux, Fogbe, by another, Bouché, it is called Jeji: another term, Ardrah, is used, but it may be presumed that one of the two last-named Dialects is intended. IV. The fourth Dialect is that of Anfúe, and is spoken in that part of the Field, which stretches from the South boundary of Dahomé towards the Atlantic, having the Weta Dialect on the East, and the Ashánti Language-Field on the West, divided from it by the River Volta. This tract was divided into several States, which have now been joined to the Kingdom of Peki. The South-West portion of Anfúe is called Krepi

&lt;!-- Page 249: Book Page 205 --&gt;

on the Map, but the real name is Peki, or Pedsi, in a North-West direction from Keta, the residence of the Mission. From this word Krepi, or Kerrapi, in itself fertile in confusion, has sprung another name, Wegbe. V. The fifth Dialect is the Anlo on the West side of the Slave Coast, bordering on Adampi of the Akrá Language-Field, but having the River Volta betwixt them, with the Atlantic on the South, the Anfúe Field on the North, and the Weta Field on the East. Schlegel has compiled his Grammar in this Dialect, and the nearest to it is the Anfúe. In spite of all the risk of admixture of foreign elements, the Anlo is the purest of the Dialects.

Many Vocabularies have been supplied: the one in Koelle's Polyglotta Africána of Adampi, a Dialect of Akrá, is in fact the Anlo Dialect of Ewé. Courdioux, a French Missionary, supplies one of Dahomé in the Journal of the French Philological Society. F. Müller gives a Grammatical Note of this Language, together with that of Ashánti, Akrá and Yariba in his Outline of Philology. Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar remarks that it has two Prefixes, and a number of Suffixes; that it resembles the Bántu Languages in Nasalization of words, Vowel syllable-terminations, and Intonation; it has neither Preposition nor Postposition, but uses Nouns to supply their place; the Genitive stands before its governing Noun, and the position alone indicates that it is the Genitive. If it is indeed a Language without Accidence, it is not so because it is undeveloped, but because it is a decayed Language.

##### *XXII. YARIBA.*

Is the Language of the Eastern half of the Slave Coast, extending from the Atlantic on the South to the Quorra Branch of the River Niger on the North, from the frontier of the Ewé Language-Field on the West to the Basin of the River Niger on the East. It is a Language which is well

&lt;!-- Page 250: Book Page 206 --&gt;

known, and the whole Field of which has been traversed, but if there is any want of clearness of idea regarding this Language, it arises from the multiplicity of Names rather heedlessly used. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gleaned from the abundance of Yariba released slaves congregated at Sierra Leóne no less than eleven varieties of names, for they cannot all be called Dialects, and recorded Vocabularies of Aku, Egba, Idsésa, Yariba, Yagba, Eki, Dsúmu, Owóro, Dsébu, Ife, Ondo. Baikie adds Ketu, Igboma and Ilórin. S. Crowther adds Ijamè, Efou, Idoko, Ayaji, Anagonu and Ayonu. Bouché adds Nago as a synonym of Yariba. Bowditch adds Hio: Donville Inongo. Clarke adds Vocabularies of Gingbe, Uieri, Uu, and Iao near Kotshi to the North. Raban gives his Vocabularies in Eyo or Oyo. D'Avezac gives a Grammatical Note of the Yebu. Sometimes Badagri is spoken of as a Language. Bunú on the River Niger, which will appear in the Niger Sub-Group with Kakanda, is notoriously a child of the Yariba. Some of the above names are Ethnological, some are Geographical; some, like Aku, are mere nicknames, from the mode of salutation; some are really Dialects. The whole are Yariba, and no other name ought to appear on the Schedule of Languages.

Bouché, a Roman Catholic Missionary, has contributed a Grammatical Note of what he calls the Nago Language, 1880, and a single Text, the result of personal local knowledge. He appears to differ considerably from all who have gone before him, of whose works he of course takes no cognizance. D'Avezac in 1841 published a Grammatical Note compiled from information gathered from a Yebu Negro at Paris. The people were not so well known then as now. Toy, a Professor in the Southern States of North America, contributed (1878) to the Transactions of the American Philological Association a Grammatical Note based upon S. Crowther's and Bowen's Grammars. Raban published in 1831 three volumes of Vocabulary and Sentences of the Eyo, compiled by him on the spot,

&lt;!-- Page 251: Book Page 207 --&gt;

as a Missionary. Pott contributed a Grammatical Note to the Journal of the German Oriental Society, based upon Crowther's Grammar. Vocabularies have been supplied from different quarters, but are superseded by regular Grammatical works. S. Crowther, a Native of the Country, and Agent of the Church Missionary Society, published in 1843 a Grammatical Note and a Vocabulary: a revised and enlarged Grammar and Vocabulary upon the same basis was published by him in 1852, with introductory remarks by Vidal, Bishop of Sierra Leóne: this was followed by a Dictionary Yariba-English and English-Yariba, by the same Author. Bowen published at Washington in 1858 a Grammar and Dictionary. Wood, after a prolonged residence, in 1879 published a Grammatical Note on the construction of the Language. F. Müller has published a Grammatical Note of this Language with that of Ewé, Akrá and Ashánti in his Outline of Philology. Lepsius has a different Classification, and groups the Yariba with the Ibo of the Niger Sub-Group, in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar. It must be added that the Yariba is the every-day Language of teaching and preaching of a large Mission at Lagos and Abeokúta. The whole Bible is in the course of publication, but unfortunately not in the Lepsius' Standard Alphabet. The Yariba people are full of energy, and from their ranks several men have already sprung up of high attainments, and we may look forward to this Language being one of the most important in Western Africa. Johnson, himself a Native of Yariba-Land, writes to me that the Yariba Language is spoken not only in its own Country, but in many places on the Niger, and in parts of Central Africa. The Yariba are a Commercial people, and very enterprizing, and they often travel long distances. There is a Yariba Colony at Kanu in Hausa-Land, speaking their own as well as the Language of the Country. They form also a large portion of the population of Loko, the Ivory Market on the Binué Branch of the River Niger.

&lt;!-- Page 252: Book Page 208 --&gt;

##### *XXIII. EBE.*

This Language is spoken North of the Yariba Language-Field on the banks of the Quorra Branch of the River Niger, to the North-west of the Nupé Language-Field of the Niger Sub-Group. S. Crowther mentions in his Journal 1859 that for the sake of comparison with the Nupé, which he was studying, he got a few words of Ebe, the bordering tribe. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary.

##### *XXIV. KAMBÁRI.*

Is still further North on the banks of the Quorra. When S. Crowther was at Nupé in 1859, he mentions that it was very amusing to hear some of his visitors join the business of compiling Vocabularies, and, when a word was given out in the Hausa Language of the Central Sub-Group, they would at once give the meaning in Nupé or Yariba, and, if their knowledge extended far enough, in Kambári also. In Baikie, the Consul at Lokója's Map, published by the Foreign Office, the name of the tract appears. At Busah, a place which will always be interesting as the scene of the death of Mungo Park, which was subsequently visited by Clapperton and Landers, it is mentioned that there is a mixed population of Borgu, Nupé and Hausa.

##### *XXV. BARBA. XXVI. BOKO. XXVII. BORGU.*

These three Languages form a little Cluster North of the Ewé and Yariba Language-Fields and South of the Quorra. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives Vocabularies of the first two, and suggests the identity of Barba with Borgu. With regard to Borgu, S. Crowther in his Journal 1859 says that for the sake of comparison, and to

&lt;!-- Page 253: Book Page 209 --&gt;

know whether Nupé stands in relationship with its neighbours, he got words of Borgu, of which Busah is a tribe on both sides of the Quorra. He states further on that there are Dialects of this Language, and that some of the tribe are Mahometan. Clapperton mentions that the Language of Busah is the same as the Language of the other people of Borgu, and appears to be a Dialect of Yariba. The value of this statement is weakened by the fact that he said the same thing of the Language of Nupé, which we know to be very different from Yariba. The name of the tract appears in the Map of Baikie above quoted.

##### *XXVIII.—XXXVIII. YOLA, KASM, KOÁMA, LEGBA, KAURE, BAGBALEN, KIAMBA alias TEMBA, JELÁNA, GURMA, GURÉSHA, MOSE.*

These eleven Languages compose Koelle's Cluster of North-eastern High Sudanian, for which Latham in his Comparative Philology 1862 substitutes the name of Kaure. If Koelle's name is too long, at least it explains what he means. Latham, in trying to be brief, has become obscure, as his name conveys no idea whatever. The Kong Mountains, of which so little is known, are at any rate the watershed betwixt the Rivers which empty into the Atlantic and into the Basin of the Quorra Branch of the Niger. In the Language-Fields of Ashánti and Ewé we have always had an unknown Northern frontier in the Kong Mountains: beyond them, and still South of the Quorra, extending up to the confines of the Súrhai Language-Field of the Central Sub-Group, is the Field of the Cluster which we now notice. When first I looked into the subject, as developed in Koelle's Vocabularies and on the Map of his Polyglotta Africána, I saw these names hovering like a covey of partridges over a totally unknown Region, untraversed by European step. I proposed to

&lt;!-- Page 254: Book Page 210 --&gt;

omit them altogether, but I found that the Language-map had provided a habitat for them all upon independent evidence. This compelled me to admit them, and I have since found some additional notices of some of them in confirmation. XXVIII. Yola. XXIX. Kasm. The thread on which these two hang is so precarious that, but for the wonderful accuracy of Koelle in many other entries, I should not have admitted them. Kasm is said by Koelle to be also called Dsindsindsin, and I find Sansan in the proper place on the Map. XXXII. Kaure, the most important of the Cluster, is evidenced by Vocabularies of Bowditch, Clarke, Kilham and Norris. The names of Dagwamba, Fobi and Ingwa are connected with it. XXXIII. Kiamba is also known as Temba. XXXV. Jelána is identified by me with Kalanna, of which Vocabularies are given by Bowditch. To any one who knows the Arabic Written Character it will appear clear how the error arose in transcription. XXXVI. Barth supplies a Manuscript Vocabulary of Gurma, and it appears on Baikie's Map. XXXVIII. Mose is evidenced by Vocabularies of Bowditch and a Manuscript Vocabulary of Barth. The name Kumshallahu is connected with it. Christaller in his Ashánti Grammar and Dictionary alludes to this Country. It is the most Northern of those mentioned by Koelle, and touches the boundary of the Language-Field of Súrhai in the great Northern bend of the Quorra.

##### *XXXIX. TOMBO.*

It is very difficult to exclude this name, which is conspicuous on the Map on the bend of the Quorra Branch of the Niger, betwixt that River and the Mande-nga. Barth in his Travels mentions it in conjunction with the Gurma and Mose. The tribe was once powerful, but has suffered much from the Fulah invasions. Barth gives a Manuscript Vocabulary of this Language.

&lt;!-- Page 255: Book Page 211 --&gt;

In conclusion, the notice of these Languages has a great importance as filling up a great blank in our knowledge. It is manifestly very imperfect, but it supplies a peg to hang further information upon. But for the Slave Trade, we should never have heard of their existence. We may expect that gradually members of these tribes will work their way from the Interior to the Coast. No doubt much more could be found out if sedulously sought for. At any rate, I have now concluded my Atlantic Sub-Group of the great Negro Group, and brought my reader down to the Basin of the River Niger. I feel instinctively that great objections may be taken to many entries in this Sub-Group, but it will only be for a time. I felt that Koelle's partridges might not settle in the Field to which the Pointer had directed my attention, and I might never get a shot at them. It is well to write about a Voice crying from the Wilderness, but in a book the purport of which is practical that Voice must come from a body with legs standing on Terra firma. Since I have completed the examination of the Language-Fields of the whole Continent, and traced so many of Koelle's partridges to their distant homes in the Negro and Bántu Field, corroborated by subsequent and independent evidence, I have conceived a great faith in his investigations. It was a book which on its appearance excited vague admiration in the unlearned, and suggested grave doubts to the instructed, but the more that Discovery has advanced, the fuller the testimony afforded to the genuineness of the work and the acuteness of the worker. It well deserved the Volney Crown, and I am trying to rouse up some one to deal with the released slaves at Frere Town and Zanzibár on the East Coast of Equatorial Africa in the same way.

&lt;!-- Page 256: Book Page 212 --&gt;

### *B. NIGER SUB-GROUP.*

Proceeding Eastward from the River Benin, the boundary of the Atlantic Sub-Group, I enter the Niger Sub-Group, which stretches along the Bight of Benin and Biafra to the Kamerún Mountains, where its Eastern frontier marches with the Northern frontier of the Bántu Language-Field. On the West it is conterminous with the Yariba Field of the Atlantic Sub-Group, on the North with the Central Sub-Group, which will be described further on. For convenience of treatment I have subdivided it into two Sections, I. Western, II. Eastern. The Western Section comprises the Basin of the River Niger, and of the Quorra Branch up to the furthest limits of the Nupé Kingdom, and of the Binué Branch up to the frontier of the Adamáwa Kingdom. The Eastern Section comprises the Basin of the old Kalábar or Cross River, and the Regions adjoining, which are very imperfectly known.

#### *I. WESTERN SECTION.*

The boundaries of the Field of this Section are on the South the Atlantic, on the North the Field of the Central Sub-Group, on the West Yariba-Land of the Atlantic Sub-Group, on the East the Eastern Section of this Sub-Group up to Adamáwa.

The frontispiece of this Sub-Group gives the likeness of a Man estimable as a friend, distinguished as a Scholar and Geographer, and valued as a Missionary Bishop, Samuel Crowther, to whom we are indebted for very much of the information which we possess of this Region. Captured as a slave from his Native village in Yariba-Land, he was released by a Captain of an English Frigate,

&lt;!-- Page 257: Portrait Plate VI (Frontispiece of Niger Sub-Group) --&gt;

**[Portrait Plate VI: Photographic Portrait]**
*S. V. White, Photo.*] [*Reading.*
"HIC NIGER EST."
### SAMUEL CROWTHER, D.D.,
Who began life as a Slave, but, through the grace of God working upon a simple and docile nature, has acquired the honoured reputation of being one of the earliest explorers of a great River, the revealer of several previously unknown Languages, the first Negro Bishop, and the first proof that Equatorial Africa can produce able, enlightened, honest, courteous, and God-fearing men.

&lt;!-- Page 258: Blank Reverse of Plate --&gt;

&lt;!-- Page 259: Book Page 213 --&gt;

landed at Sierra Leóne, where he received his education, and Priest's Orders in the Church of England. He was deputed thence to evangelize and civilize his own countrymen in Yariba-Land, and later to establish a new Mission in his Diocese of the Niger, which embraces the whole of this Section. Forty-four years have elapsed since his first visit to the Niger. Schön and Baikie have indeed greatly assisted, and many of the Bishop's subordinates, all of whom are Negroes, have co-operated, but speaking generally from the first to the last, S. Crowther's name will appear in connection with nearly every Language of the tribes who dwell on the Niger.

The history of the discovery of the River Niger belongs to this Century: its very existence was previously unknown. In 1805 Mungo Park, who had discovered the Headwaters of the Quorra Branch, known to him as the Joliba, perished at Busah, within the Language-Field of the Atlantic Sub-Group. In 1823 Clapperton worked his way from Badagri across Yariba-Land to Busah, and died. In 1830 his servant and companion, Lander, with his brother, again reached the Niger by the same route, and dropping down to the Sea reached the embouchure of the great River in the Bight of Biafra. In 1832 and 1833 Laird and Oldfield made the first ascent in a Steamer a short way up the Quorra. In 1841 the English Government despatched an expedition under Trotter, and S. Crowther and Schön were deputed to accompany it. Their Journals are of the highest value still. In 1854 Laird, under contract with the English Government, sent an expedition up the Binué Branch, and Baikie and S. Crowther accompanied it, and the latter's Journal lays the basis of our knowledge of the Languages of this Region. Since then, notably in 1857 and 1859, he has repeatedly been up and down the River, being on some occasions detained for long periods, which delays were utilized by him in studying the Languages. So great have been his services, that on my Motion in 1879 the Royal Geographical Society

&lt;!-- Page 260: Book Page 214 --&gt;

presented him with a gold watch as a token of their approbation. At the Annual Meeting Lord Northbrook the President handed it to me to forward to the Bishop, and I was delighted in 1882 to see him wearing it, and showing it to his friends in England.

In addition to the Languages which may be called the indigenous Languages of this Field, three powerful Languages of other Fields, the Yariba of the Atlantic Sub-Group, the Hausa Language of the Central Sub-Group, both of the Negro Group, and the Fulah of the Nuba-Fulah Group, as well as Arabic of the Semitic Family, are used more or less extensively. The Hausa is the handmaid of Commerce: the Fulah of Conquest: the Arabic of Religion. It is a remarkable feature how polyglot the people are. It is mentioned that the Fulah King of Nupé can express himself with equal facility in Nupé, Hausa, Fulah, and Yariba, and being an educated Mahometan no doubt he knows something of Arabic. In the streets of Igbebe near the Confluence, S. Crowther mentions that in 1854 no less than nine Languages were spoken: he names them, and they are all well-known separate Languages. Johnson, Negro Archdeacon of the Upper Niger, this very year reports that no less than fifteen Languages are spoken at Lokója, his chief residence: he gives the names, some of which are Dialects. When the expedition of 1841 went up the Niger, they had with them interpreters in eleven Languages, only one of which is a Dialect. S. Crowther stated at a Meeting of the Royal Geographical Society that in the course of a journey of seven hundred miles he came into contact with no less than thirteen mutually unintelligible forms of Speech. This gives an idea not so much of the magnitude as of the diversity within a limited area of the Languages. But the names which have appeared in books exceed the actually existing Languages, and here as elsewhere the difficulty has been to reduce them, either positively or approximately, under well-recognized general names. Many

&lt;!-- Page 261: Book Page 215 --&gt;

zealous friends of Missions on the other hand fancied that English and Yariba were the Languages of the Niger Basin.

My task has been greatly assisted by the Language-map of the Niger, prepared at my urgent request at the close of 1881 by the Bishop and his two Archdeacons: if not absolutely correct, it is a great advance towards correctness, and brings order and certainty into all ulterior inquiries. The labours of the Missionary are greatly impeded by this difference of Languages, as the Pastor of a Congregation on the Niger can no more be transferred from one post to another than can a Member of the Indian Civil Service be transferred from one part of British India to another without having his power of addressing the people destroyed. The Archdeacon of the Upper Niger mentioned in 1882 that at Lokója he preached apparently at the same time in English and Yariba. The School Master then and there took up his sentences and rendered them in Nupé, while the Native Pastor threw them off into Hausa, so as to get access to all the Congregation. In the American Colonies the class of Negroes called Ibo were well known in contradistinction to the Ako or Yariba, the Kongo, the Moko, and the Popo. Under this term Ibo were, till a very few years back, included all the Races of the Niger Basin, which no doubt are very different, having descended from different parts of the Interior at different dates.

I do not undervalue the important Journal and labours of Baikie, the Consul at Lokója, and the Journals and labours of Schön, an honoured Missionary of the Church Missionary Society; but still the clear light which we now possess is derived from the persistent and intelligent labours of Crowther, father and son, and Johnson, all Natives of Yariba-Land. It is delightful to think that in the first and second generation of released slaves men of Culture, Honesty, and high feeling of Benevolence and Devotion, should be found able and willing steadily to resist Cannibalism, Human Sacrifices, Witchcraft, and the gross

&lt;!-- Page 262: Book Page 216 --&gt;

Immorality of the Niger Races, and to lead them on gently on the path of the only true Civilization, the Christian, while lawful Commerce, conducted by the United African Steam Ship Company, brings them into legitimate intercourse with the Natives of Europe. A Printing Press at Bonny, conducted by the Christians of this Mission, tenders in good style for all kinds of printing, Invitations to Dinner, and Ladies' Visiting cards, while in the neighbourhood human flesh is still sold on the shambles. I am taking steps to supply Lokója on the Upper Niger with a Printing Press also. Active exertions are being made to reduce to writing every one of the Languages, and to publish Religious and Educational books, Translations of the Holy Scriptures, Prayers, and Hymns, at towns, where there are five hundred Chiefs, each one of whom heralds his taking up office by sacrificing the life of three slaves, and whose departure to another world is accompanied by the same atrocious formality, in spite of the remonstrance of the Christian Missionary. So extraordinary is the narrow line of demarkation betwixt abominable iniquity and hopes of better things, that the Archdeacon of the Upper Niger reports this year that at the Mission House at Onitsha he saw and reproved a young man who only the previous day had charged his own mother with witchcraft, compelled her to drink the poison-test, seen her die, and laughed at the reproof given to him. In a few years all this will pass away under the light of publicity and self-interest. I cannot forget that in British India I have associated with respectable well-educated men who have taken part in the Immolation of Widows, have steadily for many generations killed all their female Children without shame or compunction, and buried lepers alive; and yet all this has passed away under the stern discipline of Christian English Law.

It is necessary to mention this, as Language is the important vehicle by which Civilization will be conveyed to these Races. How can they be improved, unless they are

&lt;!-- Page 263: Book Page 217 --&gt;

spoken to? How can they be spoken to, unless men, capable of conveying the Arts of Peace, be instructed in their Language? How can they be instructed, unless it is found out what those Languages are, and earnest endeavours are made to furnish the material of teaching? To this one object three great English Societies, the Church Missionary Society, the British and Foreign Bible Society, and the Christian Knowledge Society, are lending their aid. It is a work of Patience, Love, and Time: the River Niger supplies a water-way, free from Cataracts, into the heart of Africa, by the Quorra Branch to Timbaktu, and by the Binué Branch into Regions imperfectly known by the exertions of Flegel. Year by year fresh Languages will come under subjection, new tribes into communication, as Commerce, and Missionary Effort, feel their way up the stream, establishing new Settlements, and organizing new Communities. The progress will be very slow, if, as now, so much has to be done by interpreters: a sermon preached through such a medium must lose nearly all its force, when it is remembered from what class such interpreters generally come, and the danger is lest a bad Creole Patois, a kind of Pidgin-Niger, spring into existence, sufficient for the rude purposes of Barter, or Service, but perfectly unfit to be the vehicle of Civilization, Evangelization, and Education, which is the nobler object of Language, and to which we are taught that every Language of the World can be adapted, if handled by an able and sympathetic Manipulator. The human string will give back every note, if properly tuned and harmoniously struck.

At the suggestion of Schön, I applied to the Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs for permission to examine the papers of Baikie, the Consul at Lokója, on whose untimely death his papers were sent to the Foreign Office, and whose name is always mentioned with reverence. By leave of the Librarian I was allowed to look through the drawers, in which Norris had left these and other papers, when he withdrew from his duties. I thus came upon the ashes, as

&lt;!-- Page 264: Book Page 218 --&gt;

it were, of two great African Scholars, and I touched their papers with reverence. Baikie's papers had been examined by Kirk, who referred me to a contribution made by him to the Journal of the Royal Geographical Society in 1867. Notice is here made of Seventeen Manuscript Vocabularies collected by Baikie. From the Foreign Office I also received a copy of some printed Reports of Baikie in 1862, and a Map, which supplied new sources of information. Nothing is sadder than the fate of the Manuscript Notes of the African traveller, unless he has been fortunate enough to get safe home and live long enough to publish them. I have looked sadly at the mouldering and all but useless papers of Krapf, Rebman, Schlencker, and Reichardt. Barth's unpublished Manuscripts I fortunately traced to their hiding-place. Occasionally the question has arisen, whether such a one had left any Notes. We know that Overweg imprudently made no Notes, and Vogel's were lost. But really, if they are buried in a Record Office or Library in England, or stowed away in a Family Cabinet in some distant English town, they are as much lost to Science as if they had been strewn upon an African Desert, or burnt in an African Camp fire, or eaten by African white ants. If any one has under his keeping packets of Manuscripts of a deceased traveller, let him at once bring them to light. In Baikie's letters to the Foreign Office he alluded to other Languages, of which he had collected Vocabularies, in addition to the seventeen quoted by Kirk, some of which I have traced and entered on my Schedule. Of the seventeen I have traced to their home ten, leaving seven still in the Limbo of uncertainty. Are they Dialects of Nupé or Hausa, or independent Languages? An inspection of the Vocabularies by Schön, or some other practised Scholar (if any exist in Europe), might solve the difficulty; but the Manuscripts themselves have disappeared. They are not in the Foreign Office, nor in the Royal Geographical Society, and as all the deceased's effects were sold by Auction, they seemed hopelessly lost.

&lt;!-- Page 265: Book Page 219 --&gt;

At the suggestion of Kirk I wrote to his sister in the Orkney Islands, and at once received a large Collection of Vocabularies, one of which bore Kirk's endorsement, showing that I was on the right track, but the much-wished-for Vocabularies were not forthcoming. A further search is promised, but I have to go to the Press with my duty unfulfilled to this distinguished Scholar. I can only record the names of the Vocabularies which were seen by Kirk, who arranged Baikie's papers after his death, and which are neither forthcoming nor identified. I. Towáni. II. Niryom. III. Kattab. IV. Wura. V. Gadde. VI. Gulenya or Agu. VII. Ungwoi. All these tribes or towns are indicated on Baikie's Map; but without an inspection of the Vocabularies or collateral evidence it is impossible to enter them on my Schedule either as Languages or Dialects. I have taken measures to have printed and circulated among all the Agents of the Church Missionary Society on the Niger (and indeed wherever they are to be found in Africa), Printed Lists of Test-Words and Sentences, compiled some years ago by the Bengal Asiatic Society for the purpose of ascertaining the Languages of British India, which measure eventuated in an admirable volume published by a late Lieutenant-Governor of Bengál. Each sheet is ruled so as to contain six Languages, and those which are cognate to each other should be entered on one sheet. Care is to be taken to exclude all Loan-words, and to enter only the pure words of the Language: one System of Transliteration is to be used by each Compiler: if he does not adopt the Lepsius' Standard, as he ought to do, he must explain his system and adhere to it. If this measure be carried out, in a few years we shall have every useful Language represented by a genuine specimen, compiled upon uniform principles, upon the basis of the same Materials, and this will prove a decided advance of our knowledge.

&lt;!-- Page 266: Book Page 220 --&gt;

The Languages of the Western Section of the Niger Sub-Group are the following:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. IDZO. | XIII. DSÁBA. |
| II. IZEKÍRI. | XIV. DOMA. |
| III. IBO. | XV. MICHI. |
| IV. KUKURÚKU. | XVI. JUKU. |
| V. IGÁRA. | XVII. BORITSU. |
| VI. IGBÍRA. | XVIII. AFU. |
| VII. KAKANDA. | XIX. MBARÍKE. |
| VIII. NUPÉ. | XX. JARÁWA. |
| IX. AWÁRE. | XXI. KADARA. |
| X. ASU. | XXII. DARÓRO. |
| XI. GBARI. | XXIII. KWANA. |
| XII. YASGÚA. | |

##### *I. IDZO or IJO, OJO, EJO, IYO, UDZO, ORU.*

Is the Language of the Delta of the River Niger. The similarity of the name had led to confusion with its neighbour the Ibo. Moreover, as foreigners ascended the different mouths, they naturally called the Language spoken by the name of the place, Bonny *alias* Obani, Okulóma and Okolobá; Brass *alias* Nempe and Itébu, New Kalabár, Akassa, Okrika, which are but Dialects (if indeed they rise even to the importance of Dialects) of Idzo. It is spoken to the extent of one hundred miles from the mouth of the Nun Branch of the River Niger: how far it extends to the Right and Left is quite uncertain, but one hundred and fifty miles at least along the Coast. Within the Idzo Language-Field many of the Ibo-speaking Races reside; an instance of the confusion is given by Dandeson Crowther, Archdeacon, in 1880. He read prayers at Okrika in Ibo, preached in Yariba, his own Native Language, but his Sermon was doubly interpreted Right and Left, Ibo to those who understood

&lt;!-- Page 267: Book Page 221 --&gt;

that Language, and in the Okrika Dialect of Idzo to the others. In addition to the above names, I read of a tribe called Egen, and another called Sobo, and a third called Ogbujan, and a fourth called Mbofia, and a fifth called Alége, speaking Dialects of the Idzo, or at least supposed to do so. Koelle, Baikie, Daniell, Clarke, Norris, supply Vocabularies under different names. Okori and Egbéle are added to the names already recorded. Gradually we shall know clearer the value of the entries. Each tribe is called by a different name by each of their neighbours: they may be mere synonyms or names of towns, or possibly totally distinct Languages. Köler, a German Doctor, in 1840 published a work of more pretence, but in reality only a Vocabulary of the Bonny or Okulóma Dialect with German renderings. Carew, a Negro Missionary, published 1864 a Primer and Texts in the Bonny Dialect, as well as Educational and Religious works. Taylor, a Negro Missionary, a Primer in the Akassa Dialect. Johnston, a Native Missionary, published similar works in the Brass Dialect. The time has come for definitely accepting one Standard Dialect, and translating a Gospel in it, which will fix it as a literary Language. It is mentioned that there is no word for Girl or Sister, and the School fees are demanded for woman-boys. This is a Language likely to develope into a powerful and wide-spread Vernacular. The Brass Dialect appears to be taking the lead, as the Church of England Prayer-book has been translated into it, and the Church Services are conducted in this Dialect. This is a great guarantee of the Dialect used being intelligible to the people. A Translation Committee has been formed of ten men and two women under the superintendence of the Negro Pastors and Negro Archdeacon for the revision of the Prayer-book, before it is printed in the local Press, and the Translation of the Scriptures will be commenced. Words and idioms are thus secured according to the usage of the Country, and as this Committee represents the

&lt;!-- Page 268: Book Page 222 --&gt;

people of the Country, whatever is translated will be thoroughly understood and subjected to a strict test. An interesting Linguistic fact came to light in the course of these operations, that the Language spoken by the people was a modern form of an older Language still spoken by some of the older people, whose brains were at once picked to collect Vocabularies, Fables, Traditions and Proverbs. The Pagan Priests use this Archaic Language in their Sacrificial rites, and it is deemed a Sacred Language. Here we have instances of two Phenomena. An old Language becoming useless and unintelligible by lapse of time, and therefore monopolized by Priests and called Sacred. Have we not heard of some such a Phenomenon in connection with Latin, Greek, Sanskrit, Pali, and Old Slavonic? The second Phenomenon is that educated Negroes are of their own accord prompted to discharge the duties of Dialect Societies and Folk-lore Societies. These little side-views of the Negro Character are most interesting, as suggestive of intellectual power and a capacity to assimilate a higher Civilization.

##### *II. IZEKÍRI or TSHEKERI, DSEKIRI, called also BENIN, BINI, IWINE.*

Is spoken in the District called Wari, to the West of the Idzo Language-Field, and bordering on the Yariba Language-Field, with no known Northern boundaries. D'Avezac, Baikie, Kilham, Clarke, Norris, Laird, Oldfield, and Koelle, give Vocabularies in some of the above names, to which must be added, under the same reservations as expressed regarding the preceding Languages, the names of Oloma, Ihewe, Obiakba. S. Crowther confessed that he knew nothing about it, and, as the Language-Field lies off the great high water way of Commerce, it is possible that twenty years hence we shall know little more than we do now, for no European establishment will ever be made in so unhealthy a tract.

&lt;!-- Page 269: Book Page 223 --&gt;

##### *III. IBO or EBOE, IGBO.*

Commences at the apex of the Delta of the Niger, occupying both banks for a length of sixty miles, with a large and unknown extent into the Interior on the Left or Eastern bank. There appear to be four Dialects, I. Isoáma, II. Elugu, III. Abadja or Aro, and IV. Abo, but the first has been accepted as the literary Standard and the leading form of speech among all the tribal subdivisions: it has a considerable extra-Territorial usage beyond its natural frontiers. The Isoáma Dialect is said by Baikie to be the most widely diffused, softest and best. S. Crowther adds that natural speakers of other Dialects will learn to speak in this, while natural speakers in this will yield to no other: hence Translations in Isoáma are universally received, but, where there are words actually different, or so disguised by change of orthography or pronunciation as to appear to be so, the Dialectal word is entered in brackets as a gloss. The danger is that names may be entered under Ibo, which properly belong to one of the other Languages, because, as already stated, all the slaves who came from the Niger were in America called Ibo, but Clarke, who supplies most, and indicates the Ibo Country, had very wide and lengthy experience, and knew what he was about. The following names are provisionally attached to this Language-Field under the usual reservations. Isiel, Apa, Uhobo, Ndoki, Okkiri, Bretshi, Enishi, Amitsh, Oss, Nkissi, Loopa, Orata, Grou, Niko, Alugieka, Oboya, Obagwa, Ogali, Ogi, Olugu, Omowo, Owa: of all these, either Numerals or short Vocabularies are supplied. Nothing but local inquiries will clear up the uncertainty, but as regards this Language much may be hoped. S. Crowther with the help of Native Pastors printed two Primers, which were superseded in 1861 by Schön's Grammar, a most satisfactory work based on original

&lt;!-- Page 270: Book Page 224 --&gt;

Materials, Translations of Common Prayer, and Matthew's Gospel by Taylor, a Native Pastor. To this has succeeded Translations of a large portion of the New Testament, and a considerable amount of Educational Vernacular literature by several Authors, all belonging to the Mission. Vocabularies had been supplied by Clapperton, Kilham, Koelle, Norris, Baikie, Laird, and Oldfield, as well as Clarke already alluded to. A copy has reached me, while I am writing this Chapter, of an Ibo-English and English-Ibo Vocabulary of considerable size, which I have assisted S. Crowther to get printed by the Christian Knowledge Society, under the Editorship of Schön. F. Müller in his Outline of Philology has given a Grammatical Note on this Language, and Lepsius passes it in Review in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar. No doubt it is the chief indigenous Language of the Niger. Schön draws attention to the existence of Tones, as differentiating the meaning of words. The Elugu Dialect is highly diluted with the admixture of the Language of the people residing on the other side of the Niger, who have migrated into Ibo-Land. Johnson writes that these Colonies come from Ado, which is the same as Kukurúku, and Benin, which is the same as Izekíri. It is clear that our information is not accurate as yet. At any rate, there are twenty large towns and villages where the people speak the Elugu Dialect of the Ibo Language, and the people who are used to the Isoáma Dialect understand the Elugu with difficulty. The Book of Common Prayer has been translated and is under revision for the Press.

##### *IV. KUKURÚKU.*

Is the name of a tribe occupying a narrow strip of land on the Right bank of the River Niger opposite to Ida. S. Crowther tells us in his books, and he repeated it to me personally, that they are so called from their peculiar habit of calling out to one another in an unintelligible sound, as

&lt;!-- Page 271: Book Page 225 --&gt;

if a cock were crowing: he confessed that he knew nothing of, or about the Language. There is no doubt however of its existence, as Johnson mentions it as one of the fifteen spoken in the streets of Lokója. It is so entered in the Language-Map of the Niger supplied by S. Crowther last year. North of Benin I find on the Map the name of Ado or Edoh, and S. Crowther in his Journal of 1854 writes of the natives of the Right bank speaking Ado, and on the Map accompanying his Journal the word Ado covers the space assigned to the Kukurúku in the Language-map, so I conclude that they are identical. Baikie distinctly connects them together. I urged upon Johnson to get some information, as obviously he has persons speaking the Language under his hand. The Country is described as hilly and lies along the banks of the great River, and cannot be overlooked. Johnson in reply to my query writes that the name of the people is Ikpere, and that they are called by their conquerors Kukurúku, because they are as easily captured as a cock. The Ikpere do not like being so called; and nothing is known about their Language, but endeavours are being made to collect a Vocabulary.

##### *V. IGÁRA or IGÁLA, and AKPOTTO.*

Is spoken on the Left bank of the River Niger, North of the Ibo Language-Field, one hundred and ten miles as far as the confluence of the Quorra and Binué, and further up inland on the Left bank of the Binué. Ida is the Capital of this Territory. S. Crowther considers it to be a mixture of Yariba, his own native Language, and the original Akpotto, the natives of Yariba having presumably immigrated and become amalgamated with the indigenous tribes, or at least the earlier arrivals. At Ala both Ibo and Igára are spoken, as the people mix one with the other on this the boundary town. It is noteworthy that S. Crowther, though a Yariba man, spoke with them through a Hausa interpreter: he gives in his Journal a few short

&lt;!-- Page 272: Book Page 226 --&gt;

Sentences of Yariba and Igára for the sake of comparison. Koelle and Clarke give a Vocabulary, and Coomber, a Negro Pastor, published a Primer and some Translations of the New Testament in 1867. This is a very fair beginning, but more must and will be done, as the Language-Field occupies a most important position, and is readily accessible. I am glad to hear from Johnson that Williams, a Negro Catechist, has collected a large number of words to form a Vocabulary.

##### *VI. IGBIRA.*

Consists of two Dialects, (1) Panda, (2) Hima. Igbira-Panda, called Koto by the Hausa, and Kotokóri by the Yariba, is spoken on the Right bank of the Binué just above the confluence, but, when their Country was overrun by the Fulah invaders, the tribe migrated across the River into the Country of the Akpotto, mentioned above as speaking the Igára Language. The original Language-Field used to extend fifty miles along the Binué, the chief town being Panda: the people are Pagan, but friendly. The Igbira-Hima are on the Right side of the Niger betwixt Ida and the confluence. Koelle and Clarke give Vocabularies, the former one of Igbira and one of Igu, the chief town. Coomber, a Negro Pastor, in 1866 published a Primer accompanied by a Vocabulary Igbira-English and English-Igbira. Williams, a Negro Catechist in charge of Gbebe on the Left bank of the River Niger below the confluence, has lately made Translations into Igbira, some of which are used in Divine Service, and thoroughly understood. Other Educational and Religious Works have been prepared, and will shortly pass through the Press. There is good hope therefore for this Language, which is important from its position. Johnson considers that the Hima is the original or "Home" Dialect, and the Panda only a Dialect of the Hima. Johnson reports that Williams has prepared a Primer, and a Translation of the Book of Common Prayer.

&lt;!-- Page 273: Book Page 227 --&gt;

##### *VII. KAKANDA.*

Is a Language presenting some difficulties in the treatment. S. Crowther remarks that the tribe has been so driven about, that it is difficult to ascertain the limits of their Territory: they used to inhabit the mountain range on the Right bank of the Quorra to the boundaries of Nupé, and here he came into contact with them when in 1871 he marched from Lokója to Bida, and in his account he has a bad word for the people of Kakanda, and in this quarter they appear in the Language-map sent to me in 1881 by D. Crowther. Then again S. Crowther in his Journal of 1854 describes the Language as being "a Dialect of Yariba," and in his Yariba Grammar as being "a child of Yariba;" but this is not quite the case. Baikie tells us that Kakanda is not the native name, and that in that term are included three distinct tribes, Basa, Ishabe and Bunú, differing from each other in Language and tribal marks. Schön in his Journal 1842 remarks that the inhabitants of the hills behind Lokója speak Bunú, and S. Crowther verbally told me in 1882 that Bunú is a corrupt Yariba, and a Primer of Bunú-Yariba was prepared by a Negro, Thomas, but not printed. S. Crowther further informed me that Kakanda is an admixture of Bunú and Nupé. Upon this basis Kakanda can provisionally be admitted as a separate Language, occupying a transition position betwixt Yariba and Nupé, which its Geographical position quite supports. The names in connection with it are Basa, Eki, probably tribal divisions; Ibbodah, the name which they call themselves; Shabbe or Ishabe, the name of the tribe or Language. Vocabularies are supplied by Norris, Baikie, Laird and Oldfield, Koelle and Clarke; but nothing further has yet been done, but I am hopeful that, as their habitat is so near to Lokója, and their Language is one of the fifteen spoken in that Market, we shall learn more about it. S.

&lt;!-- Page 274: Book Page 228 --&gt;

Crowther remarked that they all understood Yariba, which renders their claims not very urgent. Johnson mentions that at one of his Services at Lokója one of the leading women of his Congregation brought into the Church about a dozen of her countrywomen who had come from the Bunú Country. After the Service, the Archdeacon asked them to tell him something of what they had heard, and they did so, as their Language of Bunú was but a Dialect of Yariba.

##### *VIII. NUPÉ or NUPI, NYFE, NYFFÉ, NUFI, TAPUA, TAPPA, JAFFA.*

Is a most important Language-Field, situated between the Yariba Language-Field on the South and the Hausa Language-Field on the North, on both banks of the Quorra Branch of the River Niger, and composing an independent Fulah kingdom, the capital of which was formerly Rabba, and is now Bida. It has three Dialects: I. Gbedéghi, II. Bini, and III. Basa-Komi. The two former combined form the literary Standard. It has a breadth of twenty-five miles towards Yariba, and a length along the River of one hundred and sixty miles. Its Northern boundary is uncertain. The Court and many of the population are Mahometan, and the Language is spoken down the River Niger owing to the number of Nupé Slaves. At Lokója, at the confluence, Nupé is the principal Language, though others are spoken. The real difficulty is to dispose of all the names which are more or less connected with this Language-Field. Biyáni, Esitáko, Kupa, Eregba, Kamúku, Daffi, Iapa: they may mean nothing or they may mean a good deal. I must leave it to Time to decide.

The Basa Dialect of Nupé or Basa-Komi, requires a separate notice. It has no connection with the Basa already mentioned in connection with the Kakanda Language: it is spoken on the Right bank of the Binué, just

&lt;!-- Page 275: Book Page 229 --&gt;

above the confluence. S. Crowther calls it a distinct Dialect of the Nupé, and he was well acquainted with that Language, and visited the Basa in 1854. Johnson writes that it is a mixture of Nupé and Igbira. He mentions further that this Country had been overrun by the Fuláh, and that they had crossed the Binué to the Akpotto Country.

Clarke, Kilham, Koelle, Norris, Laird and Oldfield, Clapperton, Baikie give Vocabularies of Nupé, the value of which is superseded by S. Crowther's Grammar and Vocabulary compiled on the spot and published in 1864 with a Preface, in which he describes the affinity of this Language to Yariba, and the Loan-words of the Hausa. He remarks on the importance of the Tones, and the necessity of Tone-marks to prevent great mistakes in reading. F. Müller in a letter to my address remarks that Nupé is an intermediate betwixt Ibo and Yariba. In correction of the Classification given in his Universal Ethnology he proposes one Class to be called the Ewé-Ibo Cluster, or the Languages of the Slave and Gold Coast; for in this Cluster there are three subdivisions which it may be convenient to note here without comment, as my method of treatment is Geographical: I. Ashánti, Akrá, Ewé, Yariba, II. Efík, III. Ibo-Nupé. Johnson reports that he has compiled a Reading-Book and Translations of Catechisms, and that Paul, a Negro Catechist, had translated portions of the Common Prayer and the Four Gospels, which were ready for the Press.

##### *IX. AWARE.*

Baikie mentions that there was a curious tribe whose habitat was between the Bunú and Yariba, of whose Language he had compiled a small Vocabulary, which however is not forthcoming. It is duly entered in the Map, and cannot therefore be omitted.

&lt;!-- Page 276: Book Page 230 --&gt;

##### *X. GBARI or GWARI.*

Among the fifteen Languages mentioned by Johnson as being spoken in the Market-place of Lokója is the Gbari. Baikie mentions it as the largest and most important of all the Countries North of the Binué, part of it is tributary, and part independent. The Gbari slaves are very much prized. The Language is described as connected with Nupé. Baikie had collected a Vocabulary, which is however not forthcoming. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of Goali with two Dialects, which may possibly be identical.

##### *XI. ASU.*

Baikie mentions that between Nupé and Kambári is an aberrant tribe of Nupé, called by the Nupé Ebbe, and by the Hausa Abewa. The Language is nearly connected with Nupé. No specimens however have passed under my eye.

##### *XII. YASGUA or YASKWO.*

Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of this Language, and Baikie mentions that he also collected one, but it has not reached me. The habitat is well identified on the Map prepared by Baikie in one of his tours, and printed with a file of Foreign Office Correspondence, a copy of which was given me by the Foreign Office.

##### *XIII. DSÁBA.*

Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of a Language called Ham or Dsahám, called Dsába by the Hausa. Baikie mentions that he had collected a Vocabulary of the Zhába, and it appears on his Map above mentioned.

&lt;!-- Page 277: Book Page 231 --&gt;

##### *XIV. DOMA.*

Is the Language spoken by a tribe residing on the Right or Northern bank of the Binué Branch of the River Niger. They were visited by S. Crowther and Baikie in 1854, and the former in his Journal gives a considerable Vocabulary, and tells us that the tribe is named Agatu, and that they had been driven across the River by the invading Fulah into Akpotto Land: the Language has decided affinities with Yariba, both in formative prefixes, construction of sentences and roots, and probably has admixture of other Languages. Specimens of Translations were made by S. Crowther, and handed to Baikie, but I have been unable to trace them. No other Authority mentions this Language. Baikie mentions two mutually unintelligible Dialects, I. Doma, II. Arago.

##### *XV. MICHI, MIDSI, MBIDSI.*

Is the Language spoken by a tribe, dwelling on the Left or South Bank of the Binué, and is described by S. Crowther, who with Baikie visited them, as being quite peculiar to itself: the Language-Field lies to the East of Akpotto Land: on the West it has the Juku: to the South its boundaries are quite unknown: a straight line would cut the Cross River in the Eastern Section of this Sub-Group, and it seems strange that this very inconsiderable distance has never been traversed, or at least no European has come in contact with a person who has crossed. Part of the tribe are independent, and part pay a tribute to their neighbours on the East. S. Crowther in his Journal 1854 gives a scant Vocabulary, and Specimens of Translations were made, and handed to Baikie, but I cannot trace them. Bleek on comparing the Vocabulary with that of the Tiwi, supplied by Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána, discovered, that with very slight Dialectal differences they were identical. They are called Akpa or Apa by the Ibo.

&lt;!-- Page 278: Book Page 232 --&gt;

##### *XVI. JÚKU or DJÚKU, DSÚKU.*

This is the Language of the Wakári, the people of Kororófa: it is interesting, as at this point the lines drawn from Central Africa by Barth and Vogel touch those of S. Crowther and Baikie, who came from the South. The Language is also called Akpa, but the people call themselves Baibai. The Language-Field is bounded on the West by the Michi, on the East by the Language-Field of Adamáwa of the Central Sub-Group, on the North by the River Niger, and on the South by unknown tribes: a straight line drawn would reach the Kamerún Mountains within the limits of the Bántu Family. Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary. It is stated that they have been reduced to servitude by the invading Fulah, and are a leaf-wearing people. Some of them have found their way direct to Cross River, and got as far as Fernando Po Island. Baikie and S. Crowther appear to have collected no Vocabulary. Baikie in his Journal alludes to the existence of Dialects. Barth in his Travels tells us that there are certainly several distinct Dialects, that he wrote down some hundred words, which he is satisfied differed little from the Language of the Wakári, or Juku; but I have been unable to trace this Vocabulary. Koelle in the Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of a tribe called Afudu, who are Cannibals, and entirely nude, whose position seems to connect them with the Juku.

##### *XVII. BORITSU.*

Koelle in the Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of this Language, and the habitat of the tribe has been sufficiently identified North of the Binué to admit it on my list, but nothing further is known.

##### *XVIII. AFU or EKPE.*

Among the fifteen Languages spoken in the Market-place of Lokója, Johnson mentions the Afu, a name which

&lt;!-- Page 279: Book Page 233 --&gt;

was new to me. I wrote to him for further information: he fairly confessed that he knew nothing about it. Baikie, who was long Consul at Lokója, mentions it in his Grammatical Note of Hausa and Fulah, and Rohlfs on his expedition to the Binué mentions it just above Lokója, and places it in the Map of his published work with the alternative name of Ekpe, and in Johnson's Language-map I find it in the same place with the alternative name of Èpe. No Vocabulary is given, but there is doubtless a form of speech. Whether it is an independent Language, or a Dialect of some other Language, Johnson will soon find out.

##### *XIX. MBARÍKE.*

Koelle gives in his Polyglotta Africána a Vocabulary of this Language. The slave who was his informant described his journey through Igbira Panda, Igára and Ibo to the sea, leaving little doubt as to the Region from which he came. The Geographical position has been distinctly pointed out.

##### *XX. JARÁWA or DSARÁWA.*

Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána gives a Vocabulary of this Language, and its habitat is fixed on the Map.

##### *XXI. KADARA. XXII. DARÓRO or AYA. XXIII. KWÁNA.*

May be added. Manuscript Vocabularies of the first two by Baikie are reported to have existed, though they have disappeared. A Manuscript Vocabulary of the third is among the unedited Vocabularies of Barth: of all three the habitat is satisfactorily evidenced.

On the whole, this is a Region full of hope: it is the one in which the Intellect of the cultivated Negro will have free scope to distinguish itself. One German explorer has contributed much of Geographical interest, and no doubt something of Linguistic interest will be gathered

&lt;!-- Page 280: Book Page 234 --&gt;

from his Narrative when published. I allude to Flegel, who as the reward of steady and continuous labour has this year succeeded in tracing the Binué Branch of the River Niger to its source.

#### *II. EASTERN SECTION.*

It was plain sailing up the Niger in the Western Section, piloted by S. Crowther, but the navigation of the Cross River on the Eastern Section is exceedingly difficult. It presents a very narrow front to the Bight of Biafra from the River Antonio, where it impinges upon the Idzo Language-Field of the Western Section to the Kamerún Mountains, at the base of which unquestionably Languages of the Bántu Family are spoken. Following the course of the Cross River upwards, we come upon the names of Languages the existence of which is certified by the statement of Goldie, a Presbyterian Missionary, stationed many years at Duke Town, but they are names without any Vocabulary. Penetrating further into the unknown Interior, we have to find, as best we can, a habitat for tribes of which we possess Vocabularies compiled by Koelle and Clarke, both trustworthy informants. The latter indeed deserves special credit, as he resided many years at the Island of Fernando Po, just opposite, and availed himself of his great opportunities.

The shape of the Language-Field of the Eastern Section is therefore like a funnel with a narrow mouth to the Sea but widening in the Interior. Its frontier marches on the West with the Eastern Section into totally unknown Regions, and to the North it has no known boundaries, until some adventurous traveller finds his way from Michi-Land on the Binué Branch of the River Niger due South to the great Southern curve of the Cross River. To the East a great difficulty presents

&lt;!-- Page 281: Book Page 235 --&gt;

itself, for the Eastern limits of this Section of necessity march with the frontier of the great Bántu Language-Field of South Africa, somewhere along the watershed of the Romby Mountains, whence flows the Rio del Rey to the Sea. Clarke, who speaks with authority, writes that up to these Mountains the Isubu Language of the Bántu Family prevails. If we could follow the Basin of the Cross River, we might find some Ethnological frontier-line, as we do most decidedly on the Northern frontier of the Bántu Family on the East Coast, where it impinges on the Hamitic Group, but there is no such marked separation known to us on the West Coast betwixt the Bántu and Negro Languages. Lepsius, Bleek, Norris, and Logan would have it that there is in fact no such sharp Linguistic separation at all, and in fact Bleek does include the Niger Languages, Gold-Coast Languages, and Sierra-Leóne Languages in his list of Bántu Languages. Lepsius does not go so far as *that*, but F. Müller draws a distinct line, and in this work I have accepted F. Müller as my guide, without prejudice to the results of future investigators. On the Sea-Coast, as stated above, we have the Dualla and Isubu Languages of the Bántu Family sharply divided by the Rio del Rey from the Efík and its cognates of the Negro Group. From this point I have to draw an imaginary line West of the Romby Mountains till I meet the supposed upper waters of the Cross River, and then strike off North-East to the frontier of Adamáwa on the Binué in the Central Sub-Group. However arbitrary this may be, something of this kind is necessary, for just at this point another covey of Koelle's partridges are seen on his Language-map, and unlocated Vocabularies hovering over unexplored Regions. In addition to Koelle, Clarke supplies a score of names with short Vocabularies assigned in a general way to tribes living North and East of the Kamerún and Romby Mountains. My method appears to me to be the only one to secure certainty. Every one of Koelle and Clarke's

&lt;!-- Page 282: Book Page 236 --&gt;

Vocabularies are appropriated, as far as a resting-place can be found, most of them South-East of this imaginary line in the Northern Sub-Branch of the West Branch of the Bántu Family.

Baikie, who resided a considerable time at Lokója, and made a voyage of discovery up the Binué as far as Adamáwa, in one of his despatches to the Foreign Office 1862 remarks that, while the inhabitants of the Sudán were all Negro, all the tribes South of the Binué were more or less connected with the wide-spread Bántu (called by him Káfir) Race, and that the limits of the spread of the Mahometan Religion very nearly coincided with the line of demarcation betwixt the two Races. He seemed to think that the Negro mind was more susceptive of the Mahometan idea than the Bántu. At any rate, the line betwixt the Languages of the Negro Group and Bántu Family must run somewhere in this direction, and I have struck a line across without prejudice to the results of future discovery. It has caused me great anxiety, and I feel rather ashamed of this Section. There is only one Language with a Grammar and Dictionary, the Efík: all the rest are either Goldie's shadows without substances, *i.e.* Languages of unquestionable location without Vocabularies, or Koelle and Clarke's substances without shadows, *i.e.* Vocabularies without unquestionable locations.

Clarkson in his History of the Abolition of the Slave Trade mentions that as many slaves were annually deported from Bonny in the Idzo Field, and Old Kalabár River in the Efík Field, as from all the rest of the Guinea-Coast together. The consequence is that no great Kingdom exists in this quarter, but there are numerous petty tribes, each speaking their own Language. Goldie remarks that there were within a radius of one hundred miles from Creek town eight different tribes with as many distinct Languages. In the Interior there would be many more. Goldie had peculiar opportunities of noting the difference of the Languages of this Sub-Group and of the Bántu

&lt;!-- Page 283: Book Page 237 --&gt;

Family, and he had read up all the literature on the subject. He remarks that Prefixes and Alliteration were found in some, if not in all, of the Languages, and no such marked Linguistic line could be drawn as attempted, and as these tribes were all evidently of the same Race, experience would lead him to infer that these Languages must possess many affinities. There the matter must for the present rest.

The Languages are as follows:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. EFÍK. | IX. MOKO. |
| II. KWA. | X. ATAM. |
| III. ANDÓNI. | XI. OKAM. |
| IV. AKÁYON. | XII. EAFÉN. |
| V. USAHÁDET. | XIII. EKATAMFÚLU. |
| VI. U'WET. | XIV. NKI. |
| VII. UMON. | XV. MBOFON. |
| VIII. AKURÁKURA. | |

##### *I. EFÍK or OLD KALABÁR, KALBRÁ, KALABÁ.*

Is the chief Language of the Estuary of the Cross River, of which Duke Town and Creek Town are the chief places. The United Presbyterian Mission has been settled here many years, and to it we are indebted for a long series of publications, Religious and Educational, and Vocabularies.
