Koelle, Kilham, and Clarke have supplied Vocabularies. Other names must be mentioned as included in this Field under the usual reservations: Anan, Ipe, Jinimiya, Kimbo, Kosse. There are two Dialects, I. Ibóko, which is the Standard, and II. Ibibío. In 1857 Goldie of the above-named Mission published a Grammar and Texts, of which a second Edition appeared in 1868 at Edinburgh, and in 1874 the same Author published at Glasgow a Dictionary Efík-English and English-Efík, with a Preface descriptive of the locality, and the neighbouring Languages,

&lt;!-- Page 284: Book Page 238 --&gt;

and valuable remarks upon African Languages in general, and the mode of Transliteration. His predecessors Waddell and Edgerley had been at work collecting Materials since 1846: both these books have the advantage of the long local and personal experience of the compiler. F. Müller in his Outline of Philology gives a Grammatical Note of this Language, and Lepsius in the Preface to his Nuba Grammar passes it under Review: it obviously occupies an important position on the very frontier of the Bántu Family. It is essentially a Prefix-Language, but Lepsius admits that the Nominal Prefixes do not serve to differentiate Classes, as in the Bántu Family, but generally to indicate the derivation of the Noun from Verbal Roots. The Genitive follows the Governing Noun without any connecting particle: there exists a distinct Euphonic Concord in the Conjugation: there are no Tones. Of this Language we have all the information that we could desire. The Vocabulary of Anán supplied by Koelle in his Polyglotta Africána is connected with this Language.

##### *II. KWA or QUA.*

Is different from the Efík and is spoken to the South of that Language more immediately on the Coast. It is stated that the Efík are immigrants from the Interior, and that the Qua are the indigenous inhabitants: this means only that the Qua began to move down the River earlier than the Efík. Its Western boundary is said to be the River Upobo, and it extends thirty miles up the River. Baikie remarks that they are called Abakpa or Akwa or Kpekba. Goldie in his Efík Dictionary, and Robertson in his Notes of Africa, give Vocabularies.

##### *III. ANDÓNI.*

Is spoken to the West of Kwa along the Sea-Coast, limited on the West by the River Antonio. Baikie re-

&lt;!-- Page 285: Book Page 239 --&gt;

marks that beyond Okrika of the Idzo Language Andóni, a distinct Language, is spoken. This is confirmed by Köler, a German Doctor, in his book on the Bonny Dialect. This writer lived some years at Bonny, and published a Vocabulary, but he remarks that the Andóni Language is quite unintelligible, difficult to learn and difficult to pronounce. This leaves no doubt that it was not a Dialect of Idzo. Clarke gives the Numerals: there is no other specimen of the Language.

##### *IV. AKÁYON. V. USAHADET. VI. UWET. VII. UMON or BOSON or YALA.*

Are mentioned as Languages adjacent to Efík by Goldie in his Efík Dictionary. Of the first and third he gives Vocabularies; of the second he locates it near Backasey, but there is no room on the Language-map to express it. The existence of the fourth is confirmed by a Notice in the Proceedings of the Royal Geographical Society. Edgerley, who had been up the Old Kalabár River, states that his acquaintance with the Umon Language was very imperfect. As he does not supply a single word, we cannot credit him with any knowledge of it at all.

##### *VIII. AKURÁKURA or EKUNÁKUNA or AKUNÓKUNO.*

Are different names representing a Language spoken on the great Southern bend of the Cross River. The locality is identified. Koelle and Clarke give Vocabularies. Koelle writes the name also as Agurágor, which is clearly identical with the first of the three names. In the Proceedings of the Royal Geographical Society this last name is written Okúriké: these variations are the lamentable consequence of the absence of one fixed Standard of Transliteration.

&lt;!-- Page 286: Book Page 240 --&gt;

##### *IX. MOKO or MOKKO.*

This is the name by which all the Negroes who came from this Region were called in America, and subsequently in Sierra Leóne; and Koelle enters sixteen names of Languages in this Class, but gives no special Language with that name. Of these names I have been obliged to reject some, and some are unquestionably Bántu, such as Diwála and Isubu. But the name Moko appears on the Map conspicuously within the area of the Eastern Section of the Niger Sub-Group, and Oldendorp, Kilham, Norris, Clarke give Vocabularies, which it is impossible to pass over. Clarke specifically attaches his Vocabularies to a large district up the Old Kalabár River, and inland from Efík and the Romby Mountains.

##### *X. ATAM or OTAM or UDOM.*

This is the name by which the representatives of eight tribes were called in Sierra Leóne, but it is expressly stated that they do not call their Country so. It is quite possible, therefore, that the term may be a nickname, like that of Aku is of the Yariba from their form of Salutation. Koelle gives a Vocabulary of Udom, and there is no difficulty in identifying it with a Vocabulary given by Clarke of Otam with the information that it is in Moko, near to Ekunákuna, on the Cross River.

##### *XI. OKAM. XII. EAFEN. XIII. EKATAMFÚLU. XIV. NKI. XV. MBOFON.*

These five Languages are represented by Vocabularies in Koelle's Polyglotta Africána, and the habitat of the tribes is sufficiently identified. Okam and Nki appear on the Language-map, but in the limited space it was impossible to express the other three.

&lt;!-- Page 287: Book Page 241 --&gt;

### *C. CENTRAL SUB-GROUP.*

When I leave the Sea-Coast and River-Basins, and plunge into the Interior, I lose the aid and companionship of the Missionary. North of the Equator no plucky dash at the Interior has been made, such as we shall notice South of the Equator in the occupation of the Equatorial Lakes. We depend for our information, therefore, upon travellers, and fortunately a succession of great travellers have from the West, North, and East, pressed into the Basin of the Upper Niger River, Lake Tsad, and the Shari River. Among them Barth and Nachtigall, following on the steps of Denham and Clapperton, Caillie and Lang, are conspicuous. Scholars, without visiting the Region, have arranged the Materials of others, or picked the brains of Negroes who came under their influence, and thus supplied Grammatical Notes, or even Grammars: such are Koelle, Norris, and Schön. Otherwise, the Slave-Collections of Vocabularies fail us here; but on the other hand the information supplied by Barth and Nachtigall as travellers, and Koelle and Schön as Grammarians, is of the highest value and the most superior character. Another remarkable feature is that we come upon the dividing line of the Eastern and Western Slave-Trade.

Barth accompanied Richardson and Overweg in 1849. He outlived both his companions, and his unfortunate successor Vogel, who perished in the Country. Barth has left us his five Volumes of Travels replete with Grammatical Notes, and his unfinished Central African Vocabularies, Parts i. ii. and iii. I made particular enquiries with regard to the Manuscript Materials of Parts iv. and v. of that work, as in the works of Latham extracts from them are quoted, the information having been communicated in Manuscript, in anticipation of the work which the great traveller did not live to complete. An expression in vol. ii. p. 442 of Nachtigall's Sahára and Sudán roused the suspicion that that traveller must have Barth's unpublished

&lt;!-- Page 288: Book Page 242 --&gt;

Materials. After fruitless inquiry in London and Berlin, I went over to Tunis to have an interview with Nachtigall, for the scanty allusions in Barth's Travels made the absence of full details the more to be regretted. Nachtigall had after the interval of some years published vol. i. and ii. of the Sahára and Sudán, full of valuable Linguistic Notes, some of which are entirely new; but his third volume, completing his Narrative, and accompanied by an Appendix of Linguistic information, has been delayed *sine die* by the Author's departure for Tunis as Consul-General. I am too late, therefore, for some portions of Barth's collected information, and too early for that of Nachtigall. It cannot be too forcibly impressed upon travellers, that it is their duty to themselves and Science to publish at once, and not to run the risk of much of their hard-bought knowledge being poured on the ground like water. I shall ever feel grateful to Nachtigall for his ready kindness and intelligent co-operation. I had no wish to anticipate his valuable information, but I asked as a favour, and he at once acceded to my request, to be favoured with a list of the unpublished Materials of Barth, which had passed into his hands, and a specimen of which he showed me, and a list of his own Vocabularies which will be published in his Third Volume. This makes an important addition to my Bibliography of this Region, and brings our knowledge up to high level. It appears that the Concluding Remarks of Barth's Central African Vocabularies exist in Manuscript, and no less than forty-three smaller Vocabularies, the greater ones having been published in Parts i. ii. and iii. of his work. So carefully had the Field been examined before, that I find only two additional Languages to add to my list from Barth's Materials, and three from Nachtigall's. To each name in the Bibliography an entry has been made that an unpublished Vocabulary of Barth or Nachtigall exists.

It is not easy to define the limits of this vast Language-Field. To the North it reaches into the Sahára, for the

&lt;!-- Page 289: Book Page 243 --&gt;

Súrhai on the West flank, and the Tibbu on the East, extend into that Region. To the East it is in a general way limited by the watershed of the Upper Nile Basin. On the West its frontier marches with the extreme Eastern frontier of the Western Sub-Group, but can in no way be defined. To the South it extends as far as the frontier of the Niger Sub-Group already described, and the unknown frontier of the Bántu Family. The aboriginal inhabitants are generally Pagan, but Mahometan Conquerors have poured in and settled during many Centuries, and the whole Language-Field is honeycombed by the intrusive Languages of Arabic and Fulah. It is difficult to exhibit these Phenomena on the Map in Colours. Tribes speaking Arabic have been settled for many generations, in addition to the Nomad tribes always on the move: political power has passed into the hands of the Fulah Race, which is constantly encroaching. While some of the tribes are in an extremely low state of Culture, both sexes being nude, on the other hand we have the spectacle in some quarters of surprising Civilization, and of great intelligence. And, moreover, there appears to be an absence of the abominable practices of Cannibalism and Human Sacrifice, which disgrace the Western and Niger Sub-Groups, and this improvement of the Negro Race must emphatically be credited to the influence of the Mahometan Religion. Cruel they may be still, but it is not the abomination of the Cannibalism, Human Sacrifice, and Witchcraft-Murder of the Western Negro-Land.

I tried with so large an area to devise some scheme of subdivision into Sections, but I have been unable to do so. Of the fifty-nine Languages brought to book two only, the Hausa and Kanúri, have been sufficiently illustrated, and they are both wide-spread Vernaculars. Some two or three others, Súrhai, Tibbu, Maba, Bagrima, Logón, Baele, are important Languages. The Hausa Language is interesting as exhibiting Grammatical Gender in the midst of Languages where that Phenomenon is unknown. The

&lt;!-- Page 290: Book Page 244 --&gt;

greater part of the Languages are entirely worthless and insignificant. Of many we have no specimen, but we are driven to the conclusion that they are spoken, and do exist, and must therefore be recorded. There seems little chance of our knowledge being increased. The traveller, who sojourns a while, and moves on, may perhaps get a wider and more comprehensive idea of the Languages, and more particularly of the Dialects spoken; but it is the Missionary Society alone that, by a continual succession of educated men learning, teaching, and using a Language for the highest intellectual and spiritual purposes, both orally and in writing, can ascertain and stereotype the peculiar Phenomena of a previously uncultivated and unwritten Language; and this agency is entirely absent in this Sub-Group, for no Mission has been, or is likely to be, located within its limits. Let the Linguist reflect what the whole of Africa would still have been but for the afflatus of the Missionary spirit.

It is remarkable that the Language-Field of this Sub-Group, far from being isolated, as might have been expected, and uninfluenced by the Languages of superior Races, has on the contrary for a very long period been operated upon by Semitic, Hamitic and Fulah neighbours and intruders. Although unknown entirely to the Ancient World, and dimly realized by Modern Europe, until this Century, yet since the introduction of the Camel into Africa, an agency which came into motion subsequent to the rise of the Mahometan Religion, the route of the Commercial Caravan and the Pilgrimage to Mekka, though difficult, has always been open. Edrísi, Ibn-Batúta and Leo Africánus gave peeps into the Interior of a Continent, which was constantly circumnavigated, and the state of things described by travellers is far superior to that of the West Coast and the Niger Basin. The three dominant Languages of this Field are the Súrhai, Hausa, and Kanúri, very much as Leo Africánus described them three hundred years ago. Edrísi, a Spanish Moor,

&lt;!-- Page 291: Book Page 245 --&gt;

had never visited Africa, and his Arabic work is at second-hand and reflects the knowledge of his time, 1150 A.D. Ibn-Batúta, whose charming Travels are better known in English Dress, was a native of Tangiers, and described in Arabic what he saw about 1350 A.D., and he was evidently a man of acute observation, as his remarks of what he saw in British India are true to this day. To him succeeded Leo Africánus, a Spanish Moor of Granáda, driven out of Spain by Ferdinand and Isabella, a traveller as well as the Compiler of the knowledge of others. He settled at Rome, and died there in 1556 as a Christian, having written both in Arabic and Latin. He describes the Kingdoms and the Languages extending as far as the Nile. Of the South beyond the Kingdoms described he admits that he and his Authorities knew nothing, beyond a rumour that the tribes beyond traded with people who bordered on the Ocean, a significant allusion to the then budding European Commerce on the Slave and Gold Coasts, which, as a fact, was at that period just commencing.

The number of different Languages has been a subject of remarks which are worth quoting. A native of Bornu informed Lucas, an Agent of the West African Association, that there were thirty different Languages spoken in that Kingdom. Seetzen at Cairo obtained from a Negro of Wadai somewhat similar information. The names of twenty-four are given, and eleven are identified and ten appear in my Schedule. Barth remarks that as many as fifteen Languages were spoken in the kingdom of Bornu, owing to the system of Centralization, whereby several tribes had been nearly annihilated; but he does not detail the names. Barth mentions that his companion gave him a list of thirty-eight Languages spoken in the Kingdom of Fumbína or Adamáwa on the Upper Binué, and he sets out all the names with their peculiar Suffix attached to the Proper Name, meaning "Language." An analysis of this list leaves twenty-seven unrecognized. As regards

&lt;!-- Page 292: Book Page 246 --&gt;

the Kingdom of Wadai, Barth remarks that the number of different Languages spoken in this Kingdom does not exceed the number spoken in Bornu and Fumbína respectively, but the variety of elements in this very large area is very great. Barth gives a list of Languages spoken in the Basin of the River Shari, and Nachtigall greatly enlarges the list of Barth in the portions of the Field which he visited. It would have been worse than useless to set out in my Schedule all the names communicated to Seetzen, Lucas and Barth by their friends and informants. Such evidence is only hearsay, and Barth, though on the spot, does not speak from first-hand and proper knowledge. I have, therefore, only admitted on my list names vouched for by Barth and Nachtigall both as Geographers and Linguists, waiving the usual condition of a Vocabulary, out of respect to such eminent Authorities, who knew what they were about. Stanford's formidable list in the Appendix to Africa contains chiefly Tribal, and not Linguistic, names.

Peculiar difficulties have been experienced in arranging the order in which these Languages should be treated. In other cases the Coast-line or the banks of a River could be followed; but in this Central Sub-Group Lake Tsad is, as it were, the pivot, round which I have had to turn, commencing of necessity with the most Western Language, the Súrhai, and ending with the most Eastern, those in the Kingdom of Wadai. Small Linguistic Clusters are dimly indicated by the Authorities on whom I have to rely, such as the Tibbu-Kanúri, the Bornu, the Adamáwa, the Kotoko, the Massa or Musgu, the Shari, the Bagrima, the Maba. Very much more information is necessary, before any certain Classification of this kind can be arrived at. Nachtigall has not yet published his third volume with the Appendix, and he admits in his Narrative in the second volume that with regard to some Languages his Materials are scanty, and with regard to others non-existent.

&lt;!-- Page 293: Book Page 247 --&gt;

And it is clear that there must be many more Languages towards the South, of which we as yet know nothing. Betwixt the lowest point to which Nachtigall reached on the River Shari, to the Northern bend of the Kongo above the Equator, there is a great void large enough to humble us, and make us remember that in the Nineteenth Century, with all our appliances, we are as helpless as the Geographers of the time of Claudius Ptolemy. As far as we know, there are no Caravan routes across this unknown Region. We might welcome even a slave-dealer who had worked his way from Monbutto-Land to the Kamerún Mountains, or from the Binué Branch of the River Niger to the Kongo River. No covey of Koelle's partridges have settled in this drear waste. We can now fully appreciate the value of his Polyglotta Africána, when we reflect upon our utter ignorance of Regions which no doubt contributed Slaves to both the Eastern and Western Slave-Markets.

The following are the Languages:
